The failure of the csi issi t us fur to bring forth

position of the General Staff und the Inspectorate of
Iilitary Education to any far-reuchiny reduction of the

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
dlitary establishment. The Japanese Army is three-headed:
the General Staff has to do with the distribution and
strategio employment of military units, the Inspectorate of
Military Education with the training of troops, and the
War Orfice with the su ply of material and personnel. Each
organization is entirely independent within its own jurie-
diotion, but there is, quite naturally, ample provision for
cooperation and unified action among them and, conversely,
provision against action by one organization which will
↑ impair the functions of the other two. As indicated in the
outline of the reforms desired by the Minister of Mar set
forth in the above-mentioned despatch, General Ugaki would like,
among other things, to curtail the period of service with the
colors and reduce the size of combatant units; but these
desideratae touch upon the functions of the Inspector of
Military Education and the Chief of the General Staff,
respectively. Without the concurrence of these two officers,
therefore, the Minister of ar could not effeot any material
ohanges.
The Japanese Army seems to give a great deal of
oonsideration, when making assignnents, to the temperament
and charaoter of the individual officer. Most of the officers,
of course, remain with troops during the greater part of
their oareer; but the further an officer of more than usual
ability and oapaoity advances in his profession , the more
definitely does he become associated with some one of the
three administrative divisions. Each division is thus staffed
with distinot types of men. The War Office is composed
largely of officers, from the Minister down, who have a
politioal

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
political flair, as it is the War Office whioh is responsible
for proouring monies from parliament and must therefore be in
sympathetio contact with the political parties. This also
means of oourse that the War Office must keep the Army and
its polioies in harmony with opular currents of thought.
The officers of the General Staff and of the Inspeotorate
of Military Eduoation, on the other hand, are distinctively
soldiers and are preoccupied with the technical side of
their profession. In questions of the sort under discussion
there 1s apt to be a collision between the War ffioe and
the other two divisions not only because of temperamental
differenoes, but because of differences in their respeotive
points of view. The principal aim sought by General Ugaki,
who is cne of the ablest statesmen in the country, is to
offeot economies by cutting down the diversion of able-bodied
mon from productive industries as well as to decrease
national expenditures for military purposes, and thus to
cooperate with his Government in effecting the latter's
polioy of economy and retrenchment.  This would not be a
consideration, and perhaps quite naturally, to the soldier
whose sole interest is in maintaining as large and effective
a standing army as possible.
It was unfortunate for General Ugaki that when he
brought forward his plan, the Chiefs of the other two
divisions were suoh thorough exemplars of their own type.
Genoral Soroku Suzuki, then Chief of the General Staff, is a
splendid soldier and a man of great force, but at the same
time he is completely seeped in his profession. General
Muto, Inspeotor of Military Education, who resembles General
Suzuki in temperament, appears to have added his influence
to that of General Suzuki; and the two together were able
to

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
to arouse suoh general opposition throughout the Army
V that the commission had been unable to project any plan.
In February of this year, General Suzuki reaohed his
retirement age. The seleotion of a new Chief of the General
Staff, the most desirable post in the Army, is usually an
oocasion when not only rival clan influences but jealousies
between various branches of the Army come into play; but
this year these rivalries sank below the surface, and the
line of division was the proposed system of modernization.
Its opponents supported the candidaoy of General Muto,
while its advocates favored eneral Kanaya, Who was finally
appointed, presumably because or the influence of General
Ugaki with the Prime Minister, who is the nominating officer .
This outoome will undoubtedly make the carrying out of the
modernization project somevat easier, but at the same time
the defeat of their candidate has greatly intensified the
opposition of the more conservative element.
It would be safe to prediot that the successful
outcome of the London nference and the savings which will
hereafter be made possible i.. the haval estimates will soon
stimulate public interest in the feasibility of reducing the
army estimates. here is, hovever, one important point
whioh must be borne in mind, and that is that, while apan's
naval polioy is oriented on the United States, its military
polioy revolves around the military strength of Russia and
China. Japan today enjoys a wide margin of safety over
any possible threat from either or both of these Powers,
but their potential strength is so enormous that those
interested in defeating erforts to reduce the military
establishment

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.97)
-5-
establishment will have copious material for drawing an
larming pioture of the future. Another argument whioh will
undoubtedly be used by the oonservative element is the
necessity of maintaining as large an army as possible in order
to indootrinate the younger generation with the traditional
concepts of loyalty to the person of the Emperor.
This subjeot has, therefore, a great many aspects.
It is first of all a technical question: whether or not
the Army would be a more effective machine if it were provided
with more modern Weapons but decreased in size; but this is
a disoussion which will interest none but soldiers. It then
involves the possibility of giving the civilian element
partioipation in the administration of the army, and finally
it calls for a careful caloulation of what the future may
bring forth on the Asiatio Jontinent. These are all problems
of great importance. No attempt has been made herein to go
extensively into the subject, as it is now being discussed
only within fairly restricted circles; but it is believed that
the foregoing brief survey may  rove informative in the almost
certain event that it assumes important proportions as a
political issue.
I have the honor to ve, Sir,
Your obedient servant

W. R. Caatle Jr.

to the Department.
The Department will recall that as reported by the
Embassy, the Hamaguohi Government soon after its inauguration
in July 1929, announoed ten administrative policies that it
proposed to pursue. Among these policies was army r-form,
of whioh General Ugaki, inister of War in the Hamaguchi
Ministry, was an outstanding auvocate. In l92s, vhen General
Ugaki directed the Ministry of War, he ade an initiatory move
toward the modernization or the Army by abolishing four
divisions nd expending the resulting savings in new arms
and equipment.
The Commission, appointed by Ugaki to survey theland forces
of the Empire and to prepare plans for an extensive re-
organization of the Army, was composed solely of army officars.
In view of previous schemes of reor anization drawn up by
military men, it might have been easily foreseen that it was
quite unlikely that any decision vwould be made whioh would
weaken the Army. It will be recalled that in 1922 and
again in 192s, in response to a universal demand for re-
duotion in the size of the Army, considerable cuts were
made in personnel and the number of divisions were, in
1924, reduced from 21 to 17, the present number, vithout,
however, effeoting any appreciable reauction in expenditure.
On the other hand, the Navy had prepared the public mind
for reduotion of military expenditures by " concessions"
made at the Washington Conferenoe and more recently at the
London

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
Lomdon Conrerence by which enormous savings, on paper at
past, were effeoted. Similar savings on the part of the
Army have been demanded by the public, and, in spite of
past experience with military recommendations, seem to have
been expeoted as a result of the Conference., which has just
been held. How little their expeotations were justified will
be seen upon examining the report of the Comnission.
The report, as presented to the three Gen rals heading
the Army, has as yet not been given out to the publio, but
from the freedom and detail wit which it has bean dis-
oussed in the press, it may be assumed that the press accounts
of it are substantially accurate. The decisions made will
not offioially be announced until passed on by the Supreme
Military Council and sanotioned by the Emperor. ccording
to the acoounts in the Japanese press, the decisions reached
by the rmy ohiefs contemplate reducing the number f enlisted
mon by about 25,000 from the total of upproximately 200,000.
The saving thus effeoted, togetner with smaller savings in
various direotions, amounting in all to about Yen ,ooo,ooo.,
is to be applied toward increasing the effective fighting
trength of the Army by improving in mechanioal equipment.
The number of divisions is to be muintained as at present
by an adjus tment of personnel within the main units. I
understand that the military features of the recommendations
of the Commission are receiving the attention of the Military
Attaohé of the Embassy and I have, therefore, described the
report only in outline.
The

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
The important feature of this report, inasfar as the
publie is ooncerned, is the announcement that the savings
offeoted in one direction by the reduction of personnel
will be absorbed in another. Thus the popular demand for
substantial reduction in expenditure is ignored und not
a sen im actually to be saved the tax-payers.
Japanese tax-payers are noto riously long-suffering,
partioularly in regard to matters afrecting national defence,
having for years been indootrinated with the teachings of
the military classes that Japan's very life depends on her
military power. However, at this time the public had been
lead to believe, by statements of the present and inmediately
past Governments and by the strong sent iment in favor of
retrenohment prevailing throughout the country, that some
relier from the burden of military expenditure was in si ht.
Ironically enough, two days before the final meeting of the
Army ohiefs, the question or tax reduction had been disoussed
in detail at the annual conference of refectural Governors,
where, in opposition to the idea thate conomy of expeniiture
should be effeoted by reducing or abolishing bureaus in various
oivil ministries, it was agreed that reduction should first
begin with the Army and in education.
The result of the Conference had been avaited with no
little anticipation by the public. In this period of economio
depression when the national revenue hus shrunk nd when the
inoidence on the individual is heavier than ever, the burden
of the support of the great nilitary and naval organizations
is felt with unusual keenness. The news of the decisions of
the

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
the military leaders was followed by an almost unanimous
protest from the indignant press. The JIJI declares that
the vital question of the reduotion of expenaiture has been
bluntly ignored by the military, and uemands that the civil
government take & hand in the matter. It states that Japan's
expenditure on her Army is the hi hest in the world in pro-
portion to national revenue, being 28 per cent of the whole,
as oompared with 15 per cent for England; 22 per cent for the
United States; 19 per cent for France; 4 per cent for Italy;
7 per oent for Germany and 13 per cent for Russia. The JIJI
asks why the Army has been &ble to effect a reduction of only
83.6 per cent since 1921, whereas the Navy has been able to
show & saving of 45 per cent. It declares that the national
expenditure of the Government as a whole is too great for the
country's national resources and that in particular the Army
is a wearing burden of extravagance.
The NICHIN CHI is more vigorous in expressing its
dissatisfaotion .ith the coaclusions of the military leaders.
It points out that General Ugaki recently enphasized that
economio power is today the best guarantee of national
seourity, and that a state's strength is no longer measured
by the size of its army. The journal deolares that the
proposed military reform assists in no way in building up
the economio strength or the Empire and ignores economio
oonsiderations. It expresses its disgust at the lack of
sinoerity and the narrow viewpoint of the military leaders.
The HooHI states that the deoision reached is a grievous
disappointment to a harassed people. It oritioizes the
military authorities for adhering to their old attitude of
independenoe from the oivil government and for their
obsession that military considerations transoend all others.
The Osaka ASaHI deolares that it is absurd to expect
that

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
that national defence can be maintained by military expansion
while the nation is being impoverished. of what use, it asks,
will be a vast foroe expensively arned for a nation vhose
immediate problem is that of bare subsistenoe.
The impression which the foreign observer receives from
the study of newspaper reaction to the army's proposals, is that
the public of Japan, if its opinions are correctly represented
by the press, is primarily interested at this time neither in
sohemes for modernization or the army nor in expansion of the
Empire, nor is it unduly gitated by the pictures of the
potential tireat from abroad drawn b. t he professional militarist
but rather it is concerned wit. the economic problems of the
Empire, the reduction of taxation nu the solution of other.
problems of peace. The army is evidently not popular with
the publio at present which feels, in view of the severe
portion of its income in supporting a fightin rerce. There
is, to civilian eyes, no immcdiate foe to prepare against and
no need for a large army. 1he freedom with hich these opinions
are expressed by the press und the very determined op osition
to military expansion recently de.monstrated, may come as a
surprise to students of Japan who re not familiar with the
modern trends of Japnese ticuht.
With regard to the military viewpoint, aside from the
natural jealousy of the professional solaier for the welfare
of the Army, there are a number of factors whioh no doubt
give cause for serious conoern whenever talk zrises of re-
trenohment in military matters. In the first place many
Japanese believe that muoh of the influence whioh their
eountry carries in international affairs is based on military
strength. They are inolined to believe that if Japan were
to

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
to limit the size of her armed foroes in proportion to
her wealth she would have to oontent herself with the rank of
a second olass power. To military men, who regard themselves
.as the defenders of their oountry's honor as well as territory,
no saorifice is too great in order to maintain Japan's present
high prestige. They interpret their country's his tory since
the Restoration to read that military prowess has had the most
important part in the advancement of Japan to the status of
a great power They believe that unlike the Navy, which in
Japan's case is primarily needed for pur oses of offence,
Japan's Army is her guarantee of safety from attack. Japan
fears no attack by sea under present conaitions ,but there
always exists the potential tireat of & thrust at her con-
tinental possessions from Soviet Russia nd China, both of
whioh countries have greatly inc eased their land forces in
the past ten years.
Another and periaps the most important consideration
at this time for maintenance of Japan's Army, in the minus of
its leaders, is the part wnich Japan vill play at the General
Disarmament Conference at Geneva next year. Shoula too drastio
a reduotion be carried out as this time Japan would be without
a margin with whioh to bargain at the onference.
An additional argument for the army which presents itself
to oonservative civilian leaders as well as military men, is
that in those times when the nation is coabattins various social
and moral disintegrating forces, the Army stands as a bulwark of
oonservatism

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.79)
-8-
conservutism und as  reat pabili n orce  r te pulic,
wit. its cr r lolt.v i n the
Embassy has pointed out in picvious uespute es the wei ht
which this urgament carries i th conserv ive leaders.

Following from Reuter, Shanghai, March 17th:
"In view of reports from Japan that the Seiyukai
Ministry, in spite of its huge majority in the Diet, is
proving incapable of dealing effectively with the economic,
political and social crisis and that a stron political
clique is maneuvering to form a strong 'national' govern-
ment, failing which the army may be compelled to take
temporary control for the purpose of curbing a violent
reactionary government, which appears to be daily growing
in momentum and which threatens to precipitate serious
disorders, possibly leading to a facsist uprising,
oonsiderable importance attaohes to the farewell nessage
from General Shirakawa to the Japanese troops who are
going home.
This message concludes by saying: 'Conditions

at home

at home and abroad increase in difficulty day by day
and the responsibility of the Imperial troops in the
face of these circumstances is mounting. Even upon

of the Government - have to train yourselves sufficiently
to be ready to rally to the call immediately when things
should happen. Only thus can you answer to His Majesty's
reliance upon you."
For the Minister.

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 29, 1932.
Tokyo's conf id ent ial despatch No. 96
of August 13, 1932, wi th regard to extra-
ordinary military preparations in Japan
should be read in full.
(1) Material preparations. Army
arsenals are apparently working full time;
airplane factories are more active than
ever, et cetera.
(2) Psychological preparations.
Numerous artiolesintended to incite wer
fever have been published. The Foreign Of-
fice spokesman gave to the press an entirely
uncalled-for, inaccurate and pruvocative
interpretation of the Cecretary's speech of
August 8, obviously to arouse nationalistic
and/or anti-American feeling.
(3) Financial preparations. The
passage of the bill enabling the Bank of
Japan to increase its fiduciary issue of cur-
rency has already been reported.
(4) The only element remaining op-
posed to the military group is the advisers
to the Throne, Count Makino, Lord Keeper of
the Privy Seal, and Dr. Ikki, Minister of the
Imperial Household Depart ment, both of whom
are conservatives and inclined to be pacifists.
Count

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2 -
Count Makino was threatened on May 15 when
Inukai was murdered, and it is believed that
further attempts are now being made to force
him and Dr. Ikki out of office.
There is no articulate movement looking
toward compromise of the Manchurian question
nor any indication of any tendency to listen
to conciliatory advice from the League or
any other quarter.
The purpose of the strengthenin of the
war machine and of war psychology may be
to present a united, armed front in case the
decision of the League goes against Japan or
it may be to prepare a declaration of war
against China or Soviet Russia, or it may be
that the army group has a definite war in
mind - such as a drive into Jehol, partly to
cut Marshal Chang's communications with
Manchurian rebels and partly to cut communica-
tions between China and the Trans-Siberian
Railway and the C.E.R. in case of a Soviet-
Chinese combination. The Embassy states that
probably the preparations are being made for
the purpose of being ready for possible
eventualities rather than for a definite var.

SUBJECT: Extraordinary Military Preparations in Japan.

h
CONFIDENTIAL.
Department of State
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
As of interest to the Department at the present juncture,
the following brief but signifi cant summary of extraordinary
military preparations which have been and are being made in-
Japan has been compiled by the Embassy:
1.
Material Preparations:
Aocording to the Military Attache's office, the
Army Arsenals are working full time (as far as can be
ascertained); the airplane factories are very active -
more active than they have ever been; the Navy Arsenal
at Yokosuka is working day and night; the Army has
entirely

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
entirely replaced the material taken from the ar
reserves and used in t he Manahurian and Shanghai cam-
paigns, and war reserves are believed to be now above
normal; the Army has bought around 5oo motor-cars of
various types and makes aince last September; extra-
ordinarily large purchases of American machine tools
have been made for the airplane works and arsenals;
tanks and armoured cars are being built as rapidly as
possible; a chemical factory was hurriedly built in
Meguro (outside Tokyo) for materials for chemical war-
fare and is now under military guard; there has been
a very large increase during the last six months in the
imports of raw materials usable in war, such as special
steels, glycerine, oils, raw cotton, wool, ores, etc.;
the people of the cities are being urged to buy and man
anti-aircraft equipment; anti-aircraft practice goes
on daily in Tokyo; the manoeuvres this year are excep-
tional in their number and variety, emphasis being
placed on artillery manoeuvres (the Shanghai affair
demonstrated a weakness in this line); the War Depart-
ment is asking for an additional appropriation of Yen
43,000,000, ostensibly for unemployment relief but
actually for the purpose of giving contracts for equip-
ment to pri vate conoerns and thus bringing their con-
tinuing projects for the mechanization of the Army up
to date.
2.
Psyohological Preparations:
2
Numerous articles tending to incite war fever in
the people have been published, but because of the
prominence

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
prominence of the writer, excerpts are quoted from an
article written by General Araki, Minister for War,
first published in the KAISHOSHA (an Army officers'
magazine) and translated in the JAPAN ADVERTISER of
July 26 and 27, 1932:
"There is no doubt that much courage is
needed to deal satisfactorily with the present
si tuation.
"We must start with the consciousness that
we are Japanese and no other.
"....impress upon the world their (the Jap-
anese) importance as a nation.
"The Imperial Principle of the Japanese
nati on ...is.....a thing that must be propagated
over the seven seas and extended over the five
continents. Anything that may hinder its pro-
gress must be done away with even by the use of
force.
"We of the Japanese nation ....should take
the whole si tuation (the disturbed condition of
all Asia) into our own hands. We must be active,
even expending the last portion of our national
strength.
"The countries of Eastern Asia are de ob-
jects of oppression by the white peoples. This
1s an undeniable fact and Imperial Japan ipuld no
longer let their impudenoe go unpunished."
When the Commissi on of Inquiry of the Leaue of
Nations visited Tokyo in July, 1932, the Japanese press
published concerted and inspired erticles affirming
Japan's determination to pursue its "fixed polioy".
This was partly for the benefit of the Commission, but
also partly to inspire the people to defy the League if
necessary. The Foreign Office spokesman, Mr. T. Shira-
tori, released to the Japanese press on August 9th an
entirely uncalled-for, inacourate and provocative inter-
pretation of the speech of the Secretary of State before
the

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
the Counoil for Foreign Relations.
This was obviously
released for the purpose of arousing nationalistic and/
or anti-American feeling.
The people throughout Japan (even sohool children)
are being urged to subsoribe to funds for o purchasing
and presenting to the Army "patriotism" airplanes, tanks,
passenger motor-cars, armoured motor-cars, and anti-
aircraft equipment. This is partly for the purpose of
conserving Army funds and partly to enourage war-fever.
3.
Financial Preparati ons:
A bill was rushed through the last session of the
Diet, enabling the Bank of Japan to increase its fidu-
ciary issue of currency (which is based on Government
bonds and certain commercial paper) by Yen 880,000,000.
The bank can now, therefore, take up Government bonds
to the addi tional amount of Yen 880,000,000*
This was
done partly to provide funds for the Army's activities
on the mainland, and partly to provide funds for agrar-
ian relief.
The measure will provide sufficient funds
for the present fiscal year, at the present rate of ex-
pendi ture.
4.
Political Preparations:
Through the murder of Premier Inukai on May 15, 1932,
the political power was taken out of the hands of the pro-
fessional politicians and placed under the control of the
military group. At the same time the Diet was thoroughly
cowed. The only remaining opposing element consists of
the advisers to the Throne, Count Makino, Lord Keeper of
the Privy Seal, and Dr. Ikki, Minister of the Imperial
Household

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.99)
-5 -
Household Department, both of whom are conservatives
and inalined to be pacifists.
Count Makino was threat-
ened on May 15th when Inukai was murdered, and it is
believed that further attempts are now being made to
force him and Dr. Ikki out of office.
The above observations all indicate war-like prepara-
tions.
On the other hand, when we look for indioations of
a peace-seeking or conciliatory nature, we find nothing tang-
ible.
There is, as stated by Shiratori, the Foreign Office
spokesman, in discus sing President Hoover's disarmament pro-
posal, no popular sentiment in Japan for disarmament.
There
is certainly no articulate movement looking toward compromise
of the Manchurian questi on.
There is no indication of any
tendency to listen to conciliatory advice from the League or
any other quarter.
The purpose of the strengthening of the war machine and
the building up of the war psychology is uncertain. It may
be simply to present a united, armed front to the world in
case the decision of the League goes against Japan, or to be
prepared for eventualities should China or Soviet Russia
declare war. On the other hand, it may be that the aggress-
ive Army group has a definite war in mind.
.The Army evidently
plans a dri ve into Jehol, partly to cut Chang Hsueh Liang's
communications with the rebel forces in Manchuria and partly,
perhaps, to cut oommunications between China and the Chinese
Eastern and Trans-Siberian railway, in order that China may
not be supplied from Soviet Russia in case of a Chinese-Russian
combination against Japan.
Probably, however, the preparations are being made more
for the purpose of being ready for possi ble eventualities
than

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
than for a definite war, if the present or enlarged
operations in Manchuria are not onsidered as a foreign
war.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Crew.
In quadruplicate to the Department.

Copy to Berne.

*Note: Formerly the Bank of Japan could take up an
unlimited amount of Government bonds and issue currency
in payment thereof, but a tax of 5 per cent was levied on
the fiduciary currency issue in excess of Yen 120,000,000,
which made it unprofitable for the Bank to take up the
bonds. The tax-free fiduciary issue was by the Act in
question raised to Yen 1,000,000,000, thereby making it
profitable for the Bank to take up bonds and issue currency
to an additional amount of Yen 880,000,000

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.99)
APANESE MILITARY PREPARATION FOR WAR WITH SOVIET RUSSIA
FIRST - Conquest of Manchuria and Mongolia
1. Oocupation of Shanhaikwan - To hold Mrnchuria and Mongolia as a
separate antity so as to avoid any attempt for an alliance between
Manchuria and the China Proper and thus keeping Manchuria and Mon-
golia in peace.
2. Control and administer the railways in Manchuria and Mongolia for
military use and protecting transportation of military supplies.
3. Control and administer financial organizations in Manchuria and Mon-
golia where Japanese notes are circulated. The object is to bind
people in Manchuria and Mongolia economically so as to kill the
possibility of having a revolt against the Japanese and thus avoid-
ing heavy military expenses.
4. Control and administer important industries in Manchuria and Mongo-
11a.
5. Control and administer agricultural products in Manchuria and Mon-
golia.
6. Extensive suppression of anti-Japanese organizations in Manchuria and
Mongolia. Kanto(/), due to its Geopraphical position, has al-
ways been the hot-bed of Korean Bolshevik activities.
SECoND - Establishment of "Puppet Government" in Manchuria and Mongolia by
Chinese for maintenance of peace and transportation of military supplies.
THIRD - Occupation of the Chinese Eastern Railway and making it the main line
for military use and thus transferring the battle field to the Northern
Manchuria where the Japanese can avoid gorilla warfare. Secondly, should
the battle field be transferred to the Northern Manchuria, the Japanese
can count on the Southern Manchuria for the peaceful running of military
industries.
1. Occupation of Ninguta(6塔) and Suifenho() for attack-
ing Primrskaya where the Soviet Nikolsk troops are stationed. Yla-
divostok has always been a military hindrance to Japan and if Japan
should be successful in taking Primorskaya then she does not have to
worry over Soviet attack from air over Korea and Japan.
2. Occupation of Sansing( 三 女 ) for attacking the Soviet troops
stationed at Khabarovsk and Siberia.
3. Occupation of Tsitsiham with objectives to reach Heiho(. ) to
the North and Hailar( 尤) to the West for attacking Blago-
vyeshchensk and Chita respectively.

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
outn - The decisive battle, according to the Japanese plan will be fought at
a point West of the Western Khingan Mountains. Thus the summery of the
Japanese Military plan is - First, conquest of Manchuria and Mongolia
with the administration of all the raiiways therein; second, occupation
of Primorskaya and Siberia with the administration of the Siberian
Railway; and third, to engage the Soviet troops at a decisive battle
at a point West of Hailar. So far, the Japanese has almost completed
the first step of their programme and the Nakamura Affair was actually
the result of Nakamura's mission to make military maps around Khingan
Mountains.
FIFTH - Occupation of the Northern part of Sakhalin to cut oil supply for the use
of the Soviet air force.

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.98)
JAPANESE MILITARY PREPARATION FOR WAR WITH THE UNITED STATES
I. Using Formosa as the base for attacking Philippine Islanda. Occupation
of South China preventing the United Stotes from using the mainland to
launch air raids over Korea and Japan.
Construotion of New Air bases in Formosa
There are two naval bases in Formosa namely, Mako(), Bokoto Island
(易) and Kolung(基 ). At Heito(夷) thore stationed in
the airodreme one squadron of military airplanes. It has been decided and now
in the process of construction to build a new air base for sea planes at
Tamsui Beach(类 人 ) to be completed this year. The reason for con-
structing this new air base is to protect Mako and Kelung and to take offen-
sive measures against Philippine Islands. At Dainan( ) and Anping(安平)
it is also planned to build an air base for the use of both sea and land planes.
Military Supplies in Formosa
Production of food supply in Formosa is enormous and she is capable of
supporting one million soldiers. There is one natural oil well near Kinsui(K)
which can supply oils for the use of military cars. Production of coal for the
use of naval ships is no problem at all. Should Taiwan Power Plant(公)
at Nitsugotaudan be completed, the Japanese will be able to supply enormous
amount of ammunitions to their troops.
Cut
2. Occupation of philippine Islands - The reasons are: first, to The United Stetes
away from Asia all together and thus the United States will not have any footing
to start military operabions against Japan; second, to protect the Japanese
trade around the South Sea Islands; third, to keep the Jap-nese naval and military
line in safety; forth, to terminate further troubles around the South Se Islands
in future; and lastly, to force the American navy to take San Francisco and Oga-
sawara Islands route to attack Japn.
3. Occupation of the Northern Sakhalin - To obtain supply of oil fuel and protect
Japen nd Korea from American air raids by way of Alaska.
4. Dividing the Japanese submerines into two squadrons, one of which will be sta-
tioned around Chishima Islands (†岛 多 ) and the other around the South
Sea Islands. By adopting this naval tactics, the main force of the United States
navy will be forced to take San Francisco and Ogasawara Islands route. The Japnese
expect to engage the American navy in a decisive battle at a certain point South
of Ogasawara Islands:

The following item has been supplied on "flimsy":
"Foreign Military Attachés here are concerned and
894.20
many are mystified by reports of extensive military
preparations which indicate to some that the Japanese
894.20/101
Empire soon will be ready to wage a major war.
"Most of the reports can not be verified, but the
truth in some is readily apparent to military observers.
"Some factories concerned with military orders are
working day and night, according to reports received by
the various foreign Embassies here. Large quantities of
munitions have been received both from European countries
and America.
"The question the Military Attachés are asking each
other -- and, without success, the Japanese -- is why the
preparations are being made.
"Observers in Dairen reported large amounts of mili-
of

by the representatives of some manufacturers. Chemical
supplies

"The Attaohés also consider it signifioant that
materials have been ordered for oonstruotion of strategio

lines with the main rail transport lines in Manohuria.
"Among the mass of theories advanoed the following
appear worth repeating:
"1. A majority of American and some European ob-
servers, believe that those who oontrol the polioy of
Japan are preparing for a new Russo-Japanese war. Whether
Japan wants this war or not is not clear. Some of the
Attaohes think the Japanese do want it, and want it quiok,
believing they can defeat Russia and gain control of the
Amur River Valley and Vladivostok before Russia can com-
plete counter preparations including double tracking the
Trans-Siberian Railway.
"These observers argue the Japanese long have felt
they eventually must fight Russia again and that they pre-
fer to do it before the Soviet industrialization program
advanoes farther, making Russia a more formidable enemy.
"2. Some European observers and Soviet Russians here
think the Japanese preparations envisage the possibility
of the United States aoting against Japan; and cite in this
oonneotion Japanese irritation at statements of Seoretary
of State Stimson regarding the Kellogg Paot, the Nine Power
Treaty,

Treaty, and other issues.

see a remote possibility of attaok from the United States
against whioh they merely are preparing a defense.
"To Amerioan observers this argument is absurd.
Americans here see no possibility whatever of a Japanese-
Amerioan confliot.
"They point out that Japan's economio existence de-

"3. A third group of observers believe the Japanese
military preparations consist first of gathering supplies
for their very extensive military operations in Manohuria
and secondly merely to be prepared for any eventuality.
These observers do not think the Japanese intend to provoke a

oderve of the Japanese Army stated that un, for
many years past, has been preparing her army, navy and air
forces for aggressiveness and that the preparation will be
completed within six months. The general plan of this prepar-
ation is based upon an appressive po icy towerd the United
States, a defensive policy toward Soviet Russia, and a policy
of fostering disturbances and destruction toward China. At
present, Japan is as embling all hor reserve forces, selecting
06
from among them younger and stron er men for up-to-date
military training in order to be repared for war. Training
camps are now being established in cities, towns and villages
throughout Japan for the urpose of selecting end treining
young men for enlistment in the ar y. These cans are under
the command of trained officers of the regular ermy, in
cooperation with the or evizstions of reserve forces in the
respective localities. All boys over fifteen yeers of ege
are required to take a course of training in these ca ps
in order to spare them six mnths of service when they

been enlisted under provisions that will last for six years.
The Third Plenary Session of the Central Executive

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Paris, February 7, 1933.
Serial No. W. D. 1180
STRICTLY CONFIDETIAL REPORT
By Warrington Dawson
Special Assistant
SUBJECT: Japan's Preparations for War
Towards the end of the afternoon on Friday
February 3rd, I received a visit from a French
diplomat who is one of my oldest and most intimate
friends in France and whose friendly conversations
with me have supplied me with a considerable amount
of the background for the Reports I have been draft-
ing since my connection with the Embassy began. My
experience with him is that he is not only thoroughly
well informed but ponderate in his judgments and he
has invariably been sincere in his dealings with me.
He opened the conversation by asking me immediate-
ly if I knew of any developments in relations between
Japan and the United States.
I replied that I had heard of nothing new but
that our relations were very pleasant at this juncture,
as

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
as far as I was aware.
My interlocutor then asked me whether I had heard
any reason given for the policy pursued by Japan at
the League of Nations.
I replied that I had not.
After a brief moment of hesitation, he told me
that he had made up his mind to inform me concerning
a grave situation which he knew to be true, although
he was unable to give me the source and could not add
any details to the statement he was about to make.
He went on to say that Japan had deliberately
planned her policy before the League of Nations with
a view to forcing a situation which would allow her
to come out with a categorical declaration of policy.
For some time past, she has, according to my in-
formant, been preparing for war. Her aim is not,
however, the ostensible one of China, nor is it yet
the question of Manchukwo, which she considers as hav-
ing been settled once and for all.
What Japan intends to compass in the immediate
future is to get possession of the entire chain of
islands scattered along the Asiatic coast, so as to
make her complete mistress of the Asiatic continent.
The Philippines were mentioned by my informant
as

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
as being included in this plan and signifying that
Japan's present preparations for war were aimed direct-
ly at the United States.
Both the personal position and connections of my
informant, and his choice of language as well as the
reticences with which he surrounded his statements,
couched in carefully chosen words which, as he had ex-
plained to me in advance,could not be expanded, left
no doubt in my mind as to the fact that the information
given to me was known at the French Foreign Office and
might indeed possibly have emanated from there.
It is, however, obvious that the Foreign Office
would know nothing about the matter officially if approach-
ed on the subject.
I have been careful to give the time and period of
the day when this conversation occurred because it was
prior to public announcements of developments in Geneva
leading to Japan's open declaration of a warlike policy.
It was also unfortunately too late for me to connect in
any way with Friday's pouch to the Department, since the
pouch was being closed in Paris at the very hour when
the conversation occurred in Versailles. Still more
unfortunately, there is now no pouch until Thursday
February 9th so that the information will have been held
up

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
up practically a week in Paris.
It remains possible, however, that the present
despatch may reach Washington before the information
concerning the Philippines and the United States is
received from another source.
Forty-eight hours after the conversation upon
whioh I have just reported, I received a visit from
Commander Vincent-Brechignac, Curator of the Paris
Musée de la Marine, and compiler of the French statis-
tical annual on navy questions which corresponds to the
famous British annual, Jayne's "Fighting Ships." His
connections for keeping his annual up to date bring
to him information which has sometimes proved valuable
to me.
I profited by the opportunity to sound him dis-
cretely as to the plans of Japan, which on that day
had not yet been proclaimed as far as the war policy
was concerned.
He had evidently not heard of any direct threat
against the Philippines, but he told me that he believed
Japan to be considering the necessity of a war basis,
his assumption being the wish to strengthen her position
partioularly in Manchukwo but also on the Asiatio con-
tinent. He considered that Soviet Russia was her great
adversary, but he added significantly that he had been
muoh

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
much gratified to note that the United States had
maintained its full naval program. He added that
he considered this to be an absolute necessity in
view of the present situation in the Far East.
It may furthermore be of interest to note that
he directed my attention to the "World Peace" activities
of the Carnegie Foundation in Paris and the character
of their pacifist literature, saying that in his opin-
ion the policy which this institution was pursuing
might lead to embarrassing consequences for the United
States as well as for other countries.
When he said this, I thought that he was referring
to a piece of news which several French papers had pub-
lished concerning a question raised in Congress over
the activities of both the Carnegie and the Rockefeller
peace foundations. When I asked him, however, if his
remarks were based on this news report, he expressed
surprise at its appearance, saying he had read nothing
on the subject which he had not even discussed with
anyone, but that his impression was based directly on
his own examination of several tracts and books which
he had had the curiosity to look at in the library
organized

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
organized by the Carnegie Foundation.
Very respectfully,
warounglor Dawner
Warrington Dawson
Special Assistant
Copy to E. I. C.
In quintuplicate
851.9111/6a
WD/DG

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.98)
 Commal, Mukden, Manehuria, dated July 18, 1933,
 the Legation, Peiping, on the sub jeot "Mis cellaneous
Informatiom relating to the Attitude and Aotivities
of the Japanese Mili tary."
STRICTLY CONFIDENTI
MEMORNDUM
Conversation wi th a Japanese Soldier.
The information presented in this memorandum
was obtained from a young Japanese who is a graduate
of the Massachusetts Institute of Tochnology, and
who is now serving the second of his two years of
compulsory mili tary service. As he has boen a private
in the Independent Battalions of the Railway Guards
for over a year he should be well-informed on the
subjoets mentioned in this memorandum.
The efforts of the Jupanese Mili tary to suppress
banditry have been much less effective than official
ciroles and the press indioate. The "Manchukuo"
troops are cowardly and completely unreliable,
entirely lacking in patriotiam and morale, and in
battles frequently are of more trouble than help to
the Japanese.
On the other hand the bandits often fight
bravely. Sometimes they even go out of their way
to attack comparatively strong Japanese units. The
diffioulties the Japanese encounter in attempting
to exterminate bandits are inoreased by the hostility
of the native population, the impossibility of
obtaining muoh information from them, and the bandits
superior knowledge of the country, which in some
distriets is extromely rough and rugged.
If the Japanese Army is really to suppress banditry
in Manehuria within the nert few years, it is essen-
tial that the Japanese forces in Manohuria be greatly
reenforeed.
The Independent Battalione of the Railway Guard
may serve as an example of the very large bandit
infested areas assigned to individual Japanese com-
mands. Im addition to 1ts ordinary duties, the Rail-
way Qaard is responsible for the suppression of banditry
in t he easterm areas of Fengtien Provinee kmown as
"Tumgpiem" and the "Triangular Zone", in some parts of
the "Lieehsi" area, especially in the vicinity of
Newehwang: and in the Taonan-Tungliao area. The
tetal strength of the eommand is approximately
16.000 mem.
The fixed poliey of the Japanese Military is to
intimidate the mative population of Manekuria. To
seeemplisk this purpose both af fieers and men resort

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
to brutality. Furthermore it 1s not unusual for
Japanese officers to order
their men to
"beat up" Chinese. Rocently the man from whom this
information was obtained was part of a patrol
marohing along a railway line. The patrol dis-
covered an ignorant Chinese peasant on the tracks.
As Chinese are not allowed to walk on the traoks
the non-commissioned officer commanded the soldier
in question to "beat up" the Chinese. Believing
that the peasant was merely ignorant and being
naturally adverse to brutality the soldier merely
hit the peasant a few light cuffs. This infuriated
the commander who seized the soldier's rifle and
using 1t as a olub split open the peasant's head.
The Japanese advance on Isiuyen last January
affords another example of brutality of which the
same soldier was an eyewitness. During the advance
the Japanese troops came to a village in which,
they believed, bandi ts were being harboured. s
the Japanese troops came to this town many of the
villagors started to flee. The Japanese opened
fire with machine guns and mowed down the fleeing
villagers, even those who obviously were non-com-
batants.
Parenthoticclly it may be mentioned that
while I was in Hsiuyen a young Chinese woman was
shot and killed by a Japanese sentry. s she was
walking past a building occunied by the Japanese
mili tary the sentry callod to her and, in jest it
is alleged, pointed his rifle at her. resumably
by accident the soldier fired his rifle and the
young women fell in the street mortully wounded.
My informant reluctantly admitted that in
bandit suppression campaigns the Japanese Army
takes very few prisoners. More thun once he hs
been ordered to stab captured bandi ts through the
back wi th his bayonet. He also s lid that the
Army uses airplanes to bomb villages whioh it sus-
peets of harbouring bandi ts. Fervently he insisted
that the Army tries not to bomb innocent villages.
Airplanes, he admitted, play an extremely important
part in bandit suppression oampaims.
He ocmplained about the brutality and hard-
ships of life in the Japanese Army. Frequently
for minor offenses soldiers are "beaten up" by
their muperiors. the food is very poor and foroed
marshes are mads which, even after making allow-
amses for natural exaggeration, seem scarcely
credible. This young man admitted that he dis-
liked the Army intensely but he estimated that
only about three soldiers out of a hundred feel
about it as strongly as he does. He admitted
that he, himself, did not understand what the
Ampy's Manehurian poliey was and said he believed
very few of the soldiers tried to understand it.
They

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
They were stuffed with propagande, striotly
disoiplined, and foroed to work so hard that they
never felt up to trying to think.
Monroe Hall
Amorican Vice Consul.
American Consulate General,
Mukden, Manohuria,
July 18, 1933.

EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Tokyo, November 1, 1934.
No. 1031.
Subject:
War Office Pamphlet.
 FE(281)

COPIES SENT TO
ASSISTIN7
The Secretary of State,
C
Washington.
DEC:01934
FILED
151934
LED
ARY
STA
D
theiPress Section of the Japanese War Office issued a
EE
pamphlet entitled "Kokubo no Hongi to Sono Kyokwa no Teisho",
which can be translated in various ways but which means "A
Dissertation on the Basic Principles of National Defense and
Means of Strengthening Such Defense". The Department has
undoubtedly received press summaries of the pamphlet, but
in order that the Department may have the full text on file,
the Embassy has had the pamphlet.translated. A copy of
this

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
this translation is enclosed herowith.
The pamphlet is tio last of : series, numbering some
twenty during the past two years, issued by the Press Sec-
tion of the War Office. The others have dealt with various
phases of Japan's international and domestic relations, but
have attracted no gr at attention as they were educational
rather than suggestive of reform of the national government.
About 160,000 of each issue of the pamphlets are printed
and are distributed through Governors of prefectures, Army
units, young men's societies, etc.
The most rocent pamphlet deals with the various elements
which go to make up the defense of a nation, the effect upon
Japan's national defense of present-day international rela-
tions, and suggestions for enhancing and coordinating Japan's
defenses. It holds out the United States as Japan's possi-
ble enemy on the sea and Soviet Russia as Japan's possible
enemy on land. It also states that "If either the British
or Dutch should obstinately carry on their unjust competition
to the last(referring to the restrictive measures placed
upon the importation of Japanese goods into India and the
Netherlands Indies), the Empire may be obliged to resort to
arms for the rectification of such an attitude". However,
these open or veiled threats against other countries are not
unusual and attracted little attention. What did attract
attention was the open advocacy in the pamphlet of a sort of
state socialism, although that term was not used. The advocacy
of state socialism is implied in such phrases as the following:
"So long as economic profits, and especially
unearned incomes, are enjoyed by one section
of the nation alone, the majority of the people
will be left in utmost misery and distress..."
"The nation should therefore be freed from
economic ideas based on egoism and individual-
1sm and be awakened to general economic ideas
based

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
based on ethics . . ."
"But the situation is such that prompt mea-
sures must be taken to re-examine all the national
organs from the viewpoint of national defense
and to readjust fundamentally all policies on
finances, economics, diplomacy, education, etc."
The pamphlet also advocates the placing of newspapers, maga-
zines, radio, etc., under governmental control in peace
time, in order to provide propaganda organs. It criticizes
the present economic system, which permits improper and un-
stable prices for agricultural pro ucts, compels unduly
high prices which the farmers must pay for fertilizers and
other needed goods, and imposes heavy taxes upon agricultural
comnunities, resulting in their heavy indebtedness. The
pamphlet says little that is specific; it avoids concrete
instances and expresses the Army's ideas in rather vague
generalities. However, as Dr. S. Washio points out in the
JAPAN ADVERTISER of October 6, 1934, the correction of the
economic mal-adjustments of Japan,as pointed out in the
pamphlet, would require a revolution, and this is the point
which immediately caught the attention of the Japanese nation.
The reaction to the pamphlet was so great that it caused a
considerable decline in the Japanese stock market, the fi-
nanciers apparently fearing that the Army intended to put
into imnediate effect some of the reforms which were advo-
cated in the pamphlet.
The principal issue seems to be the extent to which the
Army should interfere in political affairs. The Army is
enjoined by Imperial Rescript from participation in the
political affairs of the nation, and the political parties
especially

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
especially were angered by this obvious attempt on the
part of the Army to advise regarding or to dictate the
domestic policies of the country. The Cabinet also was
reported to be very much disturbed over the scope of the
pamphlet, the Ministers feeling that the military, under
the guise of national defense, had taken it upon themselves
to discuss an offer a vice in regard to all national pro-
blems. The Ministers resented the encroachment by the
military upon their particular provinces, and are reported
to have expressed the opinion that there are "limits to the
competency of the military".
The political parties, it is reported, are generally
in disagreement both with the contents of the pamphlet and
with the idea that the military are competent to issue such
a document. It is believed in some circles that the pamphlet
was issued by the Army as p opaganda against the political
parties. The two major political parties, the Seiyukai
and the Minseito, for some time past have been quietly spread-
ing the opinion that the military are taking too large a share
of the revenue of the country, and that as a consequence
there is little or nothing left for agricultural relief.
The
Army, therefore, has published the pamphlet in question,
which not only puts national defense forward as the primary
duty and aim of the nation, but also advocates fundamental
changes in the economic system, designed to afford permanent
relief to agricultural communities. However that may be,
it is rumored that the political parties intend to bring up
the matter of the issuance of the pamphlet in the coming
Diet

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Diet session. One political party, however, supports
the pamphlet. That one is the Kokumei Domei, which has
since its inception advocated  sort of Fascism an which
states that the pamphlet is a reflection of its own views.
The Minister for War, General Hayashi, happened to be
away from Tokyo, on an inspection trip in the Kansai, when
the pamphlet was issued. It was thought for a time that
the younger, more ra ical element in the Army hal taken ad-
vantage of the Minister's absence to issue a pamphlet with
which he would not agree. On h's return to Tokyo, however,
he made the following pronouncement, according to Rengo
(JAPAN ADVERTISER translation):
"I am given to understand that there
has been considerable reaction to the
pamphlet published by the press section
of the War Office during my absence
from Tokyo. The aim of the press section
is to widen public knowledge about national
defense, and pamphlets with this purpose have
been published by it from time to time. The
recent pamphlet was meant to make the public
see that defense is not limited to armaments
but has inseparable associations with various
social problems.
Study Left to Others
"The pamphlet lists various matters that need
to be studied in the light of national defense,
but the actual study and application of the re-
sults of it rest with the Ministe"s directly con-
cerned with the matters in question. The army
itself does not mean to carry out what is mention-
ed in the publication. Thus the conjecture that
it means to is entirely erroneous.
"Because the pamphlet was issued during my absence
from Tokyo and on the eve of the extraordinary
session of the Diet. I am given to understand
that there is suspicion in certain quarters that
behind it is some special reason. I assure you,
however, that it was printed purely as a part of
the routine work of the press section and that
there was no ulterior motive behind it".
The

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
The most important phrase in the War Minister's statement,
to the Japanese, was to the effect that "the Army itself
does not mean to carry out what is mentioned in the publi-
cation". This assured the people that the Army had no de-
sire to stage another "May 15th Incident".
In the meantime the Army spokesman had set forth the
Army's viewpoint. This was to the effect that the economic
status of the farming population has a vital bearing on
national defense, and in this particular moment of emergency,
the military leaders regard it as quite natural for the
Army, which is responsible for the defense of the nation,
to draw the attention of the public to what the Army con-
siders is needed for the welfare of the farming communities.
Most of the newspapers have either ignored the pamghlet
or have been very non-committal in their comnents. This was
undoubtedly due to the f ct that very few of them dare to
incur the displeasure of the military. The Tokyo JIJI, how-
ever, on October 3, 1934, gave as 1ts editorial opinion
that "The Army pamphlet is a poor remedy for the misunder -
standing among some of the public regarding the enormous
fiscal demands of the Army". The KOKUMIN on the same day
stated that
"The pamphlet is the sort of pronouncement
which cannot but arouse attention and surprise
the general public. Its issuance by the Army
is without precedence and is certain to develop
into the hottest issue we have faced in years.
A report says that the Government is dismayed
by the action of the military authorities in
issuing the pamphlet. Unfortunately we cannot
dismiss the report as unfounded."
The Tokyo ASAHI, on October 6, 1934, explains the cause of
the furore created by the pamphlet by the fact that there
18

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
1s uncertainty in the public mind, both at home and abroad.
It states that "it is open to doubt that a country, faced
with crises at home and abroad, will eve! be in a position
to carry out reforms as radical as those advocatec in the
pamphlet", and decries the manner with which the Army's
views were presented to the public. The MIYAKO on October
8, 1934, warned the military against excessive advance.
The HOCHI on October 9, 1934, blamed the military, because
of their pamphlet, for causing the fall in the stock market
and denounced the principles advocatec therein as impractical.
Dr. T. Minobe, a professor of the Law Department of the
Tokyo Imperial University and an authority on international
law, in an article published in the November issue of the
CHUO KORON denounces the War Office pamphlet as being ex-
tremely militaristic and as being illogical throughout. He
decries the idea propagated in the pamphlet that national
defense is the only organ of national life which can bring
a peaceful and happy existence to the nation. He points
out that the pamphlet regards war as inevitable, without
taking into consideration the resources of diplomacy, and
denounces the ultra-nationalism expressed in the pmphlet.
He states that the War Office, in opposition to the Empire's
unalterable policy, ignores treaties, contradicts the Em-
peror's messages, and adores and inspires war. It is seldom,
in present day Japan, that one reads such strong condemna-
tion of the military as is expressed in Dr. Minobe's article.
At

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
At the present moment, the furore over the War Office
pamphlet seems to have died down, but it is possible that
1t will be revived when the Diet session convenes.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew
