=== Page 205 === (confidence 1.00)
TRANSLATION
PRINCIPLES OF NATIONAL DEFENSE AND
SUGGESTIONS OF HOW TO
STRENGTHEN
THE
SAME
By the Section of Newspapers and New Agencies,
The Department of War.
October 10, 1934.
This pamphlet is a sister pamphlet of "Japan in
Rapid Progress and Pressure by Other Powers" which was
published by the Departnent of War on July 28, 1934.
It is aimed at clarifying principles of national defense
and making suggestions for strengthening the same, and
is distributed among the people in the hope that they
will be in readiness to face any emergencies under the
extraordinary situation today.
Contents
(1)
Re-examination of ideas of national defense.
(2)
Essential elements constituting the strength
of national defense.
(A)
Human elements.
(B)
Natural elements.
(C)
Mixed elements.
(3)
International conditions at present and Japan's
national defense.
(4)
Suggestions for materializing plans of national
defense.
(A)
Systems of national defense.
(B)
National defense and domestic problems.
(C)
National defense and thoughts.
(D)

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
(D) National defense and military
strength.
(E) National defense and economics.
(5) Preparedness of the people.
(1) Re-examination of Ideas
of National Defense
Meaning of strife.
Strife is the father of creation and the mother of
culture. The struggle of individuals to stand strain
and of States to predominate over others in competition
will doubtless prove a stimulant for the development of
individuals and States alike as well as for the growth of
culture. Strife in the present instance does not mean
a barbarous fight between men and between States. A
barbarous battle is the outcome of aggressive schemes
and similar ambitions, and is diametrically opposed to
the ideals of the Japanese people whose duty it is to
encourage the growth and development of all living things
of the world. All efforts to proceed along the path of
justice and uprightness by brushing past all sorts of
barriers in the form of aggressive ambitions and thereby
to bring about an era of peace and tranquillity so as to
conform with the "king's way" of the Empire of Japan is a
mission of the Japanese people and a responsibility placed
on the Imperial army. To attain this goal through strife
as above referred to is a mission of Japan's national
defense.
Meaning

=== Page 207 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
Meaning of national defense.
National defense is an action fundamentally vital
to the growth and development of a State. The organiza-
tion and operations of a State and its social life in
such a way as to enable them to utilize the whole vital-
1ty of the State as far as possible shall be the objective
of plans of national defense.
Change of ideas of national defense.
The views above mentioned signify the meaning of
national defense viewed from the standpoint of national
defense in modern times.
The ideas of national defense have passed through
the following three stages during a period from ancient
times up to the present:
(1)
Idea of national defense from a military
standpoint.
Prior to the outbreak of the World War, national
defense was practically confined to military equipment
for the express purpose of deciding the fate of a war by
means of arms only. Naturally armies alone were engaged
in a war, while nations had indirectly participated in
national defense by supporting armies in various ways.
(2)
Idea of national defense from the viewpoint
of a general mobilization.
The remarkable development of science and tech-
nical knowledge, coupled with complexity in international
relations, has inevitably enlarged the scope of war, in
which battles are to be fought along with diplomacy, econom-
ics, thoughts, etc. For the purpose of emerging victorious
from a war, it is deemed essential to control the above-
mentioned

=== Page 208 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
mentioned elements for the launching of war and to make
necessary preparations in time of peace. This is what is
now known as a general mobilization with battles as the
key-note since the termination of the World War. Under
general mobilization, armies and nations are to parti-
cipate in a war as one body. This idea of national
defense is now beginning to be appreciated by intelligent
classes of the Empire.
(3)
Idea of national defense under the existing
situation.
The idea of national defense mentioned in the
preceding second item, however, must undergo re-examina-
tion.
The world is now suffering from economic depression
and complexity in international relations which are the
outcome of the World War. In the field of politics and
economics, nations stand against each other by forming
themselves into different blocs. The international struggle
for existence is thus becoming more intense than ever
before. Even in time of peace, economic war and "war
of thoughts" are raging furiously everywhere the world
over. Unless the whole life of a State should be adjusted
and controlled externally, the State will be left behind
as a straggler in international competition as well as
actual warfare. Such is the situation today. Naturally
the ideas of national defense have undergone a remarkable
change. By discarding the idea of relying upon actual
battles in deciding the fate of a war, it is considered
Most urgent to formulate new ideas and plans radically
different from those of the past.
Two

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Two phases of national life.
Generally speaking, national life can be observed
from two standpoints, namely national life in peace and
national life in competition.
A study of relations between national defense and
individual life will easily convince everyone what are
the relations between national defense and national life.
Individual life, like national life, has two phases,
namely peace and competition. Peaceful individual life
in a State in modern times is, to a certain extent, ad-
justed and maintained under the influence of morals and
laws. On the contrary, competitive individual life cannot
be guaranteed by relying upon strength of the other par-
ties. An individual must improve his own lot himself.
In fact, competitive individual life is guaranteed by
the application of the combined force of the physical
strength, energy and intellectual power of each and every
individual.
In peaceful national life, there exists an inter-
national morality but this is not so strict and precise
as in the case of individual life. International law
also exists but there is no super-State power to enforce
it. In other words, there is not even a single organ
to guarantee peaceful national life. This is highly
regrettable. Under the circumstances, peaceful national
life will be obliged to guarantee its existence by its
own efforts.
After

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
After the termination of the world war, the League
of Nations was organized with the object of guaranteeing
the safety of peaceful national life. Witn the progress
of the times, however, the League exposed its inability to
carry on its functions effectively. Every person now
admits that the plan for the maintenance of international
peace through the medium of the League was a mere dream.
Even peaceful national life is not guaranteed by the
strength of other parties. It is, therefore, but natural
that national existence in competition is in no way
guaranteed.
For the purpose of pushing forward competitive
national life, it is essential to operate with the con-
bined national strength as in the case of individual life
which requires the combined force of physical strength,
energy and intellectual power as mentioned above. To
speak more plainly, the operation of national defense
strength is imperative for the creation and development
of national existence. Actual warfare is not the only
objective of national defense which is composed of vitali-
ty, motive power and other elements. In international
life, it is highly important to consider that national
defense is aimed at the application of fundamental
vitality for the growth and development of a State. At
a time like the present when international competition
is at its zenith, national defense is absolutely essen-
tial in order to exalt ideals of the Empire on the one
hand and to emerge victorious from competition on the other.
Absoluteness

=== Page 211 === (confidence 1.00)
-7-
Absoluteness and relativity of national defense.
In so far as relativity of national defense is con-
cerned, it is necessary for a State to strengthen its
national defense according to conditions of other coun-
tries. As for absoluteness of national defense, there
is of course no room for discussion.
The world situation today is such that the Empire
is, under pressure brought to bear upon it by other
Powers, obliged to push forward its ideals with a strong
determination. At no time since history has been written
has the Empire felt so urgently the necessity of strengthen-
ing its national defense as at present. The latent power
of the Empire in its national defense is quite sufficient
to tide over the extraordinary situation today and this
is in practice testified to by the fact that other Powers
feel great uneasiness as to future activities of the Em-
pire. The question is how to utilize this latent power
systematically as an influence under the existing situation.
It will be a difficult task to relieve the masses
suffering from economic depression and to devise appro-
priate measures for tiding over the extraordinary situ-
ation today and at the same time to enhance the standard
of living of the people through the operation of organs
now in existence, thereby ensuring the future of the
Empire. But the situation is such that prompt measures
must be taken to re-examine all the national organs from
the viewpoint of national defense and to readjust funda-
mentally all policies on finances, economics, diplomacy,
education

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
education, etc., and then to control and operate the
latent power, spiritual and substantial, of the Empire
to the maximum extent. This would doubtless be a way
of facing and overcoming the extraordinary situation.
Quite recently the view was expressed among certain
sections of the people that international disputes may
be solved by diplomatic means. Such a view is an idle
dream of those who do not understand world affairs to-
day and is apt to mislead the people.
Use of national defense strength.
There are two methods of utilizing national defense
strength, namely negative uses and positive uses.
Negative uses.
Negative uses signify that a State, by assuming a
strong and imposing attitude, is able to attain its
objective negatively without unsheathing the sword. In
the initial stage of the Manchurian incident which oc-
curred in September 193l, Soviet Russia which was push-
ing forward its five-year plan at the time refrained
from launching any movement against Japan, due to the
adequate military equipment and the consequent imposing
attitude of Japan. Secretary of State Stimson's in-
timidations failed to be realized, due to the existence
of Japan's naval force. All this will easily convince
everyone what national defense is. Candidly speaking,
negative operations of national defense signify the
equipment of naval, military and air forces. This is
the reason why the United States of America is most eager
to

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
to acquire and maintain a powerful naval force superior
to Japan and why Soviet Russia is eagerly bent upon the
organization of an invincible army. In other words,
the United States with the support of its powerful navy
intends to insist upon the maintenance of its Monroe
Doctrine and the principles of open door and equal op-
portunity in China.
For the purpose of acquiring a diplomatic voice in
dealing with questions of the Far East, it is absolutely
necessary for the United States to have a powerful navy
sufficient to overpower the Japanese navy. From Japan's
standpoint, it is alike absolutely necessary for Japan to
have a powerful navy sufficient to defeat any State which
will attempt to prevent Japan's noble object to make the
Far East a paradise of peace and prosperity.
Soviet Russia possesses a powerful red army, through
whose medium the Soviet Government is now trying to
Bolshevise the whole world. Soviet Russia now regards
Japan as a potential enemy. It is, therefore, hardly
necessary to point out that Japan is in urgent necessity
of replenishing its national defense strength in order to
prevent Soviet Russia's scheme to encroach upon, and Bol-
shevise, the Far East.
Positive uses.
Positive uses signify the practical use of national
defense. If negative uses should fail to attain the ob-
ject to defeat its opponent and thus encourage the latter
to challenge Japan to fight, a war would then break out.
As

=== Page 214 === (confidence 1.00)
-10-
As already mentioned, economic war, war of thoughts,
diplomatic dealings, etc., are as important as actual
warfare in defeating an enemy thoroughly, although ac-
tual warfare constitutes the backbone of a war.
Why was Germany defeated in the world war? Even
in actual warfare, Germany suffered a defeat. Viewed
from a certain angle, however, Germany might be regarded
as having maintained the position of a victor to the last
moment in so far as actual warfare was concerned. Through-
out a period of five successive years, Germany did not al-
low its enemy to enter its territory even an inch, a fact
which is really praiseworthy. Germany's defeat was large-
ly due to economic blockade enforced by the Allied Powers
and the consequent internal strife among its people.
For the positive use of national defense, it is
absolutely necessary to control all important elements
of national defense and operate them as an inseparable
unity. Various Powers are now eagerly bent upon the com-
pletion of such a process as above indicated. The fate of
future wars depends upon the organization and control of
elements necessary for national defense in an efficient
manner. In short, wars in modern times may well be said
to be a contest of capabilities of organization.
Autonomy of national defense.
National defense is, in a word, fundamentally vital
for the growth and development of a State. Nothing can
be more unjust and improper than to enforce different
ratios upon various countries in proportion to the wealth
and

=== Page 215 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
and size of their territories. The autonomy of national
defense rights is an unshakable maxim the world over. An
attempt either to restrict or prohibit military prepara-
tions under international treaties on the part of various
Powers is nothing but a scheme to acquire a superior
position in national defenses under the disguise of peace.
History proves this. Even an advocate of internationalism
cannot deny this fact. Negative uses of national defense
are aimed at preventing the outbreak of a war as already
mentioned.
An ideal of law-makers is the creation of a pacific
era in which the enforcement of any law is not needed. In
like manner, an ideal of military men is to refrain from
using arms. In other words, if negative operations of
national defense should prove successful in attaining a
State's object nothing can be more ideal than such an
operation.
After the close of the world war, many wars have
since occurred in various quarters of the world. The
recent outburst in Austria might have developed into a
second world war. The irrationality of new boundary lines
in Europe, the unfair distribution of territories among
various Powers, racial prejudice, economic and financial
depression, commercial and tariff war - all these facts
are enough to convince everyone that the outbreak of a war
is inevitable sooner or later. Under the circumstances,
Japan today is in urgent necessity to equip itself fully
against emergencies that may arise at any moment.
(2)

=== Page 216 === (confidence 1.00)
-12-
(2)
Necessary Elements for
Organization of National
Defense.
Necessary elements of national defense embody all
elements constituting a State. For convenience's sake,
necessary elements may be classified into human elements,
natural elements and mixed elements.
Human Elements.
Human elements are the most important of all the
elements constituting national defense strength. Need-
less to say, human elements are a combination of spiritual
power and physical strength. In the struggle for exis-
tence on the international stage, an earnest will for
the maintenance and promotion of justice and upright-
ness as well as confidence to emerge victorious from
struggles comprise the principal substance of human ele-
ments. The conception that a victory will only be won
by those who are just and reasonable and who have a strong
determination to win is a faith of military men. This
theory may also be applied to competitions among nations.
Fostering of human elements.
The following will show how to foster human elements:
(1) To entertain ideals of the establishment of
the Empire and confidence for the fulfillment of the
noble missions of the Empire.
To foster a sentiment to relieve those who entertain
an incorrect humanitarianism and nationalism and who are
suffering from the evil effects of philosophy, religion,
arts, etc., and to realize heavy responsibility for the
maintenance

=== Page 217 === (confidence 1.00)
-13-
maintenance and protection of the Empire at this juncture
and then to fulfill this responsibility thoroughly.
(2) To foster a noble spirit of self-restraint and
to sacrifice oneself for the sake of the Empire with a
view to developing the State and to attain unity of sen-
timent by ejecting such internationalism, individualism
and liberalism as ignore the existence of a State or
States.
A noteworthy fact is that some of the Powers today
are, by means of propaganda, trying to spread anti-
national principles among nations as above indicated and
thus to attempt an alteration of the organization of
States, to bring about a split between armies and peoples,
and to force one's father-land to sustain a defeat in a
war with a view to avoiding international conflicts and
to secure the position of a victor in the end. To im-
prove the organization of the Empire and to control
national spirit for the purpose of national defense is
an important policy which should be put into force without
a moment's hesitation.
(3) A healthy person entertains wholesome ideas.
It is scarcely necessary to emphasize that great stress
should be laid upon the importance of developing the
physique of the people who recruit soldiers, the latter
of whom constitute the principal element in actual warfare.
A policy of developing the physique of the people is of
first importance in order to enable them to operate
successfully in the field of international cultural
competition

=== Page 218 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
competition on the one hand, and on the other, to with-
stand a long-term economic blockade in time of war. If
the physique of the people should deteriorate on account
of material civilization, that would be a serious question
which cannot be overlooked from the viewpoint of national
defense.
(4) In order to enable various classes of the
people to fight for the sake of the Empire by sacrificing
their own interests, it would be necessary to take measures
whereby the stabilization of living of their family mem-
bers at home is guaranteed. It will thus be seen that
the relations between national defense and national poli-
cies in general are inseparable.
As mentioned in a pamphlet entitled "Soviet Russia
viewed from the Standpoint of National Defense in Modern
Times" which was made public by the Department of War
on May 10, 1934, the organization of national defense of
Soviet Russia today is on a grand scale and it should be
noted that the zeal with which the Soviet Administration
1s now pushing forward its plans for national defense is
really astounding. However, the abuses of the Soviet
Administration, coupled with the defects of Communism,
have precipitated the masses into a state of poverty,
while various classes are suffering from instability of
living. Naturally the people are devoid of national
spirit. Because of dissatisfaction and discontent on
the part of the masses, the unification of various classes
to act as one body in time of national emergency is out of
the

=== Page 219 === (confidence 1.00)
-15-
the question. Such a defect as this is proving a barrier
to the Russian people's entertaining faith to be victorious
in war. By means of propaganda declaring that Soviet
Russia is not afraid of the so-called "yamato-damashii"
of Japan and that the Japanese people are losing national
spirit, the Soviet Administration is now trying to en-
liven the martial spirit of its people. Such a policy
1s sufficient to prove that the Russian people are
devoid of national spirit.
The conclusion is thus reached that measures should
be taken for the stabilization of national life in order
to foster the spirit of nationalism and to strengthen
the people's faith to be victorious in future inter-
national conflicts. Above all, security of living for
the working classes and the relief of agricultural com-
munities, etc., are considered most urgent.
Question of Population and Races
Population problem.
The population in Japan proper is estimated at
65,000,000. The figure wil1 reach 92,000,000 when the
population in Japan's overseas territories is added. If
Japan should cooperate with Manchukuo in national defense,
there would be a combined population of 120,000,000 al-
together. A combination of Japan and Manchukuo will con-
stitute a powerful State which may well compete with the
United States of America and Soviet Russia. The combined
State is really in an advantageous position in so far as
human

=== Page 220 === (confidence 1.00)
-16-
human elements are concerned.
Racial problem.
Soviet Russia embodies more than 180 different
tribes who stand against each other. Ukrania. which con-
tains a population of 30,000,000 souls is always looking
for an opportunity to declare independence.
How Germany is suffering from Jews who do not as-
similate may be seen from Hitler's rigid policy against
Jews. The United States of America also embodies differ-
ent races. The existence of 12,000,000 black men in the
United States is an eternal cancer.
An attempt to encourage races or tribes in a State
to be at variance with each other and thus to bring about
the collapse of the State is one of the strategies in
"war of thoughts" when nations are actually engaged in war.
Viewed in this light, racial problem cannot be treated
lightly from the viewpoint of national defense. Atten-
tion should be paid to the following points:
(1) To study thoroughly the psychology of races and
to govern and control them without committing an error.
(2) To imbue thoroughly the spirit of "king's way"
(Imperial principles) and to strengthen the sentiments
of nationalism.
(3) To contrive plans so as not to be victimized
by enemy's schemes to bring about a disruption of races
in a State.
Natural

=== Page 221 === (confidence 1.00)
-17-
Hatural Elements
Territories.
The area of territories, geographical position, area
of lands under cultivation, coast lines, boundary lines
and relations with neighboring countries have an impor-
tant bearing upon national defense. Especially the
geographical position of territories is highly important
in actual warfare and economic war as well.
The Empire of Japan is situated in the eastern
extremity of the Far East. On eccount of this advan-
tageous position, the Empire naturally enjoys the advan-
tage of carrying on a mission as a guardian of peace in
the Far East from a military and political standpoint.
Situated, as it is, in a position where there are China,
India, the South Sea Islands, etc. which are regarded
as granaries of the world and which contain a population
of 1,100,000,000 souls, the Empire is in constant com-
munication freely with those countries by water. This
fact apparently proves the keynote for the Empire's
economic development in the future. The position of the
Empire which is encircled by waters has merits and demerits
in national defense. The fact that Japan has Soviet Russia
in the west and the United States in the east will have an
important bearing on its national defense. Aerial naviga-
tion has recently attained a development to the extent
that many bombing aeroplanes capable of flying over a
radius of more than 1,500 kilometers are manufactured and
operated

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
-18-
operated. By air the Empire is now exposed to an attack
from aeroplane-carriers in the seas and from Vladivostok,
Shanghai, the Philippines, Kamchatka and the Aleutian
group on land. The Empire is, therefore, in urgent need
to provide a powerful air force.
Resources.
Measures should be devised for the cupply ano replen-
ishment of resources in ti e of war. At the same time, it
would be necessary to obtain resources with vhich to deal
with economic war and to withstand econonic blockade. As
regards resources, the folloving points should be con-
sidered:
(1)
Investigation of resources.
(2)
Storing of resources necessary for war.
(3)
Fostering of resources.
(4)
Opening up of resources.
Mixed Elements
Economics.
Economic war has come to play an important role in
war since the world war. The words "national defense" in
this pamphlet signify a struggle for existence in time of
peace as well as preparations for actual warfare. Economic
war may be regarded as constituting the principal portion
of a war. No one can, therefore, deny that economics con-
stitute an important element in national defense. As for
economic strategy, nothing is noted in this pamphlet.
Fundamentally

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
Fundamentally speaking, it is well to carry on free trade
externally. At a time like the present when virious
nations are facing each other through the medium of blocs
and are beginning to adopt a protective policy in foreign
trade, the Empire will be obliged to formulate policies
necessary to face such a situation. If any country should
adopt a policy to control exports and imports mutually and
to place certain restrictions upon quantities and prices
of merchandise and to enforce unlawful tariff rates against
other countries, the Empire would be obliged to take re-
taliatory measures.
It is well for any country to supply superior articles
at low prices for the sake of consumers the world over.
In this respect, a new industrial country like Japan where
the status of living is comparatively lov will enjoy great-
er benefits, while the old industrial countries like
England and Holland are placed in a very disadvantageous
position. The British and Dutch peoples, for the bene-
fit of a small number of governing classes are trying to
supply a large number of colored races in their terri-
tories or colonies with their manufactures at high prices.
Such a policy is irrational. On the contrary, the poli-
cies of the Empire coincide with the interests of the
consumers of the world. Viewed from a moral standpoint,
there is no doubt that the Empire will be a victor at the
last moment. If either the British or Dutch should ob-
stinately carry on their unjust competitions to the last,
the Empire may be obliged to resort to arms for the
rectification

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
rectification of such an attitude. From an internal
standpoint, economics are an important factor for the
maintenance and fostering of elements necessary for
national defense and actual warfare. Accordingly economics
are are indispensable as spiritual elements. A consider-
able amount of money is needed for the adequate replenish-
ment of materials for national defense and for the ele-
vation of the standard of living of the people. Under
the extraordinary situation today, prompt measures should
be taken for the improvement and adjustment of economic
organizations so as to bear all necessary expenditures.
By the cooperation of Japan and Manchukuo, it is
possible to produce resources which are quite sufficient
to withstand international competitions of any sort. The
Japanese people are generally diligent and industrious in
all phases of activity and have thus a unique vitality.
To adjust and utilize this vitality and resources to the
maximum limit is one of the important functions of the
Empire to face an economic war which is more than probable
to arise at no distant future.
Technique.
The development of science in modern times has made
1t possible to precipitate a war between countries which
was thought impossible in former days. A war today is
not confined to battlefields only. Bombs will be dropped
on non-combatants at home the moment a war is declared.
A future war is a struggle between all elements of nations
concerned. It is also a competition of intellectual
faculties

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.99)
-21-
faculties of nations. Arms of new type to be used in
initial stages of a future war will soon be found to be
less effective and be laid aside as old-type arms. A
nation who possesses greater creative faculties will be
a victor in a future ar. In the initial stage of the
world war, no one thought of the use of tanks and
poisonous gas. At the present time, the use of a death
ray apparatus and the like is not an idle dream.
The reason why Japanese merchandise is now consumed
in every corner of the world is superior technique, al-
though the fall in the value of the yen is one of the
causes. It is highly desirable that the technique in
the field of economic should attain a further develop-
ment. From the viewpoint of national defense, it is also
desirable that matters relating to scientific researches
which are not yet placed under control should soon be
adjusted and controlled. Furthermore, the State shall
encourage inventions by supplying adequate funds and
providing necessary apparatus, etc., for use, while the
present patent system shall be improved.
Military Strength.
Military strength or force of arms constitutes the
keynote of national defense. For the purpose of attain-
ing the object of national defense, it is thought ad-
visable that the Imperial navy should soon abrogate the
Washington and the London Naval Treaties which are dis-
advantageous to the Empire and thus secure an autonomy
in

=== Page 226 === (confidence 1.00)
-22-
in national defense and provide an adequate force for
a further development of the Empire.
As for the Imperial army, it is considered most
urgent to replenish the military strength for the pur-
pose of maintaining the position of the Empire which is
contiguous to Soviet Russia whose Government is now
eagerly bent upon the expansion of its army. It is also
urgent for the Empire to enlarge the scope of its air
forces as far as possible and thereby to remove menaces
from all directions. Private avi tion organizations
are as important as military air forces from the view-
point of actual warfare. It should be noted that the
military strength of the Empire is a sane, powerful force,
with which to crush evil actions and heretical doctrines,
and thereby to declare the Empire's law of justice before
the world. This military force radically differs from
that of other nations who use their military forces for
the realization of their selfish purposes and who are
always eager to attain a superior position in all spheres
of activity.
Communications and Propaganda.
Communications play an important part in wars as
well as cultural competition. Especially is this the case
with systems of propaganda which has an important bearing
upon the fate of a war. In the world war, the propaganda
on the part of Germany was overshadowed by the propaganda
of Great Britain and France. The consequence was that
Germany

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.99)
-23-
Germany was declared as an aggressor based on Imperial-
1sm. Germany was thus made a target of criticism fron
the rest of the world and had to suffer a defeat in the
war.
In the Manchurian incident, the Empire failed to
convince the whole world that it had taken up arms for
the sake of justice and uprightness, due to awkwardness
in propaganda. The Empire was thus obliged to withdraw
from the League of Nations. In fact, propaganda is a
sort of warfare whereby to crush enemies without shedding
blood. It is a matter of profound regret that even in-
telligent classes still fail to recognize the practical
value of propaganda.
Propaganda may be carried on through the medium
of newspapers, magazines, correspondence, pamphlets,
lectures, radio, moving pictures, exhibitions, etc., all
of which should be placed under State control in time of
peace with a view to providing for an actual war.
As regards elements for national defense, there are,
besides those mentioned in this pamphlet, many elements
which have no direct relations with matters noted in
this pamphlet and which are excluded accordingly.

(3)
) International Conditions at Present
and Japan's National Defense.
1..
World Unrest and Japan.
World unrest and Japan - The 1935-36
Crisis - The Naval Conference and
America - The attitude of China-
Withdrawal from the League and Mandated
Territories - U.S.S.R. and Far Eastern
Policy - Measures to counteract the
emergency.
The difficulty of liquidating the economic wastage
of the World War and the unreasonable provisions of the
Versailles treaties were the causes of unprecedented
political and economic inecuality and instability. All
countries, whether they participated or not in the Great
War, incurred its effects equally, irectly and indirectly,
and today the whole world groans under depression and
unrest.
The Powers, anxious to escape from this world-wide
distress, vied with one another in jettisoning idealistic
international cooperation an? turning to practical nation-
alism, to the ruin of that machinery of peace which had
controlled the world for a time following the Great War.
The whole world indulged in dirty political and economic
competition and countries grouped themselves in blocks
around the major Powers with others having the same in-
terests.
During this period Japan was also drawn into the
vortex, but on the other hand, by liquidation of unsound
undertakings, rationalisation of industry, etc., laid the
preparations for her progress in the future.
By

=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.99)
- 25 -
By chance, the Far Eastern situation grew more
critical. The Manchurian incident broke out and, because
of the boycott on Japanese goods in China, Japan was
forced to secure new markets. Thanks to the depreciation
of the yen, Japanese goods flooded the markets of the
world (China excepted) like the waves of the sea, and
brought an unprecedented trade boon to the Empire.
With the advent of Manchukuo, Japan established her
standing in Eastern Asia and the effect of the Japanese-
Manchurian alliance was to shed a wide ray of hope on
the future of both countries.
Because of this the Powers, groaning under economic
depression ani alarmed by the instability of the interna-
tional situation, each jealous of Japan's economic progress
and fearful of her accrudescence of political power, used
every means to bring pressure against her rapid political
and economic advance.
Unless the present state of affairs is transitory,
there is a danger that Japan - her goods ousted from every
available market, her colonizers excluded from every
available land, politically isolated and without friends -
may sink to the destiny of a second Germany.
The Empire is confronted by the preliminary skirmish
of such a crisis, to wit the so-called 1935-36 crisis.
The 1934-36 Crisis:
The Naval Conference and America.
Whatever sacrifices Japan may make at the Naval Con-
ference, which is to open next year, she must at all costs
secure

=== Page 230 === (confidence 0.99)

secure autonomy of national defence, and definitely can-
not tolerate a treaty on the rotio principal such as here-
tofore.
As has been stated already, national defence is a
fundamental vital function in the creation and develop-
ment of a nation an? consequently it is categorical that
outside interference can positively not be tolerated. Nor
can the dignity of an independent nation tolerate such a
thing as the imposition of ratios. Furthermore, upon the
strength or weakness of our naval forces depends the solu-
tion of the so-called problem of the Pacific an the suc-
cess or failure of our China policy.
There is not time to discuss the reason for this here
in detail, but to put it shortly, it is because America
wishes to maintain a navy definitely superior to Japan,
in order that, possessing the actual power to make it pos-
sible for her to attack and defeat the Japanese Navy, she
may be able to enforce support of her China policy. This
is no arbitrary conjecture. Commandant Ebury has made
the following public declaration:
" A defensive flcct is sufficient to up-
hold tie Monroe doctrine, but to carry out
the policy of the Open Door in China, an
offensive fleet is necessary."
The attitude of China.
China, true to her traditional policy of playing off
one country against another and failing to understand
Japan's genuine intention of furthering peace in the Far
East, has chosen the policy of continually invoking the
a1d

aid of the Powers in order to molest Japan. The princi-

rian incident by appeal to thc Leaguc.

Such a conspiracy would, in the end, lead China astray
from her true path and would leac the Far East into disorder,
and could not be countcnanced by Japan. But such a con-
spiracy woulc be openly avowed or not according to whether
Japan's naval strength was inferior or not to America's
naval strength; as is apparent when we consider that, follow-
ing the last Naval Disarmament Conference, on every occasion
when Japan has suffered ooercion from England and America,
anti-Japanese activities have broken out in China and it
has been necessary on each occasion to despatch troops.
Thus it may be said that the acceptance or otherwise
of

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.99)
-28 -
of Japan's claims at the forthconing Naval Conference
will provide an in ic tion of China's attitude to ar's
Japan hereafter, ani that whether peace can be maintained
in the Far East or not depends solely upon the result of the
Conference.
Withdrawal from the Learue and Mandatory
Administration.
Japan's withdrawal from the League will finally becone
effective in Merch of next year. The League having had its
value called into question through its participation in the
Manchurien incident has no desirc to be entangled in the
present question and consequently it may be seen that China's
plotting along traditional lines will cause no consicerable
repercussions, but it cannot be guaranteed that the related
question of mandatory a ministration vill not be brought up.
Now, the mandatory administrations werc decided at the time
of the Peace Conference by a general conference of the
former Allies, and were not mandated by the League. Conse-
quently Japan, even after her withcrawal, has legal grounds
for perpetual tenure, so that we e there to be any plan to
take them back, Japan would be justified in resisting, even
by force.. Therefore, in so far as Japan shows firm deter-
mination, there seems to be no need for anxiety.
U.S.S.R. and Far Eastern Policy.
In the next place, we must refer to relation with Soviet
Russia. The bearing upon this country of recent conditions
in Soviet Russia have already been discussed in detail in
"The U.S.S.R. from the viewpoint of modern national defence"
and

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.99)
-29 -
and need not be repeated here.
In a word, the Second Five Year Plan will be com-
pleted in 1937. With the exception of Japan and China,
Soviet Russia has contracted pacts of non-aggression and
conventions of definition of aggression with all her neigh-
boring countries. Having caught the attention of the world
she has witnessed the matcrialisation of her entry into
the League, and latterly has planned the concluding of an
Eastern Europe Locarno pact. Having thus at length alle-
viated her troubles in the West, she will now, mustering
her full strength, press forward to the execution of her
Far Eastern plans.
As is already well-known to al? the world, Soviet
Russia, with a population of 160,000,000, is equipped with
76 divisions, 1,300,000 soldiers anc 3,000 aeroplanes. For
a population of 120,000,000, Japan and Manchuria possess
together not more than about 300,000 men and 1,000 aero-
planes. And it would be difficult to foresee how many camps
the RedArmy will have in readiness by 1937. Furthermore there
are already in the Far East more than 200,000 men, 600 aero-
planes, 1,000 military automobiles (incluing tanks) and about
20 submarines, a line of modern permanent fortresses has been
prepared along the frontier and the perfection of most tho-
rough war preparations is planned. (200,000 men 1s about
equivalent to the entire strength of the Russian Army at
the battle of Shaho in the Russo-Japanese war).
On the one hand the recent succession of incidents on the
Soviet-

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)
- 30 -
Soviet-Manchurian frontier and the outrageous nature of
plots to overthrow the Japanese Special Administration
and to destroy railways, and on theother Soviet propaganda
among their own people of the powerlessness of the Japanese
army and their endeavors to implant confidence in assured
victory, make one suspicious of the real nature of their
intentions.
Unless Japan now reinforces her military strength,
especially the strength of her air force, in opposition to
this enormous red army, we are ill assured that there will
be no cause for regret in the future. Above al!, the necessi-
ty of reinforcement of her military strength in Manc uria
admits of no argument.
Measures to counter:ct the emergency.
It is possible that the intc.national circumstances
which encompass Japan may lead to a head-on collison with
England an America at the 1935 Naval Conference or there may
be extreme tension following a breakdown of the Conference.
This difficulty may well be the turning point in deciding the
future success or failure of Japan an  the issue of peace in
the Far East. The naval demands for autonomous national
defence, in order fully to safeguard the national defence,
must be fulfilled at whatever cost and the coming international
crisis must be met with firm determination.
Further, the swift advance of the whole strength of
Soviet Russia towards her Far Eastern programme must have a
great influence upon our Manchurian policy, and according to
circumstances

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.99)
- 31 -
circumstances there is no telling when conditions may
not compel us to employ measures of self-defence. While
every effort must be made to avoid this, should the chal-
lenge come preparations must be made to meet it decisively.
The reinforcement of our military equipment and the enlarge-
ment of our air force are matters of urgency and, together
with the Naval Question, are absolutely indispensable re-
quirements of national defence.
In the next place, there is the economic war with Eng-
land and other countries. The "block" economic policy has
become more and more decp-rooted, and in order to seck in
the Far East the solution of the economic deadlock in Europe
and America, it must be surmised that the Powers will des-
cend upon the Chinese market. Consequently we must trans-
cend all question of individual interests and in real unity
as a nation put into practice a plan of economic an: commer-
cial control and devise a plan to break through the crisis
by obtaining new markets and regaining our old ones in China.
To sum up, the present emergency is not one derived from
any cause which could be dispelled by concerted diplomatic
action; notwithstanding the tremendous efforts made by
every country since the Great War, it is a world crisis pre-
destined by fate, and, with the Manchurian incident and with-
drawal from the League as the turning point, it is an emer-
gency affording an ordeal of glory to Japan. We must be on
our guard against any disposition to put off the evil hour
by adopting easy expediencies to avoid or dispel the issue,
but meeting gladly the chance which fate has offered, must
use firm determination and courage in establishing a long-
lasting policy of state.

(4)
Suggestions for Materializing
Plans of National Defence.
1.
Organisation of natlonal defence.
Wars of the future will be wars of
intellect and of organisation. The
meaning of national defence plans.
As the result of recent progress in science and arts,
warfare as an international struggle for existence is be-
coming extremely scientific and systematic; and this is
more especially the case as regards conflicts of ideas,
economic conflicts an? military conflicts.
To put it briefly, it may be said that intern: tional
struggles in future will be between intellect and intellect
and between system an? system. The country with originali-
ty and organisation superior to those of other countries
will. therefore be crowned with victory. Hence, a policy
of national defence may be summed up as a policy for the
organisation and use, for the purpose of the defence of the
country, of all the factors of national defence. The fac-
tors of national defence have already been pointed out, and
may be divided as regards their use into the following cate-
gories; viz, statecraft, thought, military strength and eco-
nomics. With regard to the organisation and use of the
factors of defence, there are different opinions; but it is
thought that the following are the most valid.
2.
National Defence and domestic problems.
Stability of people's livelihood;
regeneration of agricultural and
fishing villages, - reasons for the
exhaustion of agricultural villages
and policy in regard thereto-
organisation of originality and
invention.
(1)

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.99)
- 33 -
(1) Stability of people's livelihood.
With a view to cooperation on the part of the whole
nation and in order to increase and foster the personal
factor there must be happiness for the nation at large. So
long as economic profits, and especially unearned incomes,
are enjoyed by one section of the nation alone, the
majority of the people will be left in utmost misery and
distress ani this will at length culminate in class rivalries.
This is a problem which cannot be overlooked either from
the point of view of the national policy, or, more especially,
from that of national defence.
The nation should therefore be freed from economic
ideas based on egoism and individualism an be awakened
to general economic ideas based on ethics with a view to
progress being made in the speedy establishment of an economic
organisation which will be in accordance with the ideals of
the Empire. All those interested - scholars, businessmen,
Government officials or private individuals - must unite
in the search for a policy and in plans for its materialisa-
tion.
As regards the livelihood of the people the most press-
ing problem is the relief of agricultural and fishing villages.
(2)
Regeneration of agricultural and fishing villages.
Many reasons are given for the poverty and distress of
agricultural villages at present; but the principal ones are:-
(i) Improper and unstable prices of agricultural
products.
(1i) Insufficiency of the system of distribution of
products.
(1ii) Defects in the method of the management of
agriculture and improper use of superfluous
labor.
(1v)

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.99)
- 34 -
(iv) Problem of tenancy.
(v)
Heavy burden of taxes in agriculture villages
and increase of debts.
(v1) High cost of fertilizer.
(vii) Insufficient circulation of funds in agri-
cultural villages (accumulation of capital
in towns).
(viil) Sud len fall in the price of cocoons and silk
thread.
(1x)
Natural calamities such as drought, floods,
typhoons, snow, and injurious insects.
(x)
Mistaken idea of looking down upon agriculture
and shortage of leaders among the peasantry.
(x1)
Limited area of arable land and excess of popu-
lation.
These,complicated the one by the other, are the causes of
the misery which the peasants are now experiencing; they
are mainly the result of rivalries between cities and towns
and agricultural villages. While, therefore, a social poli-
cy for the relief of agricultural villages from their desti-
tute oondition is of urg nt importance if they are to be
rescued, it is also necessary to devise a fundamental policy
for the improvement of economic organisation, and for a so-
lution of the problem of population, and other measures,
based on the principle of mutual reliance between towns and
agricultural villages co-existence and co-prosperity. It is
earnestly hoped that agricultural villages may be thoroughly
regenerated by their own self-disciplined industry and the
development and intensification of their creative powers.
(3). Organisation of originality and invention.
Originality and invention are of course important items
in national policy, while in connection with national defence
they have also an important significance, both from an
economic

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.99)
- 35 -
economic and a military point of view, since, as has already
been pointed out, future wars will be a struggle between
originality and intellect.
of the
The whole/nation's faculties of originality and inven-
tion should therefore be mobilized and scientifically or-
ganised so as to display the utmost efficiency. For this
purpose (1) scientific research organs should be brought
under a single control and rationalised, their efficiency
should be increased, economies effected and their employ-
ment facilitated; and () invention should be encouraged
and the patent system improved by the provision of funds
and by facilitating the use of research organs.
3.
National defence and thought.
Conflicts of 1deas and perfection of
the personal factor; principal points
in national culture; system in a con-
flict of ideas.
The importance of conflicts of ideas in connection
with national defence has already been explained. Such
conflicts, moreover, are based on such personal factors
as mental and physical powers. These powers, therefore, must
be cultivated by school education and social training, while
at the same time, by the correction of social defects and
the adjustment of the economic system, the stability of
livelihood of the nation and the regeneration and relief
of agricultural villages must be promoted with a view to
increasing the national resources.
From the point of view of national defence, the important
points to be considered in connection with measures to
counter a conflict of ideas are as follows:-
(1)

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.99)
- 36 -
(1) Promotion of national culture.
(1) A firm belief in the ideals of the Empire and its
mission must be implanted an sound national and ethical
1deas, such as are in no way affected by the prevailing
unrest and radical thought, must be firmly established.
(ii) A noble spirit of self-sacrifice for the cause
of the country as a whole must be fostered, and international-
ism, egoism and individualism, which lead to forgetfulness
of the state, to evasion of control essential to the state
and to conduct contrary to the interests of the country,
must be eradicted.
(iii) A snirit of sincerity and strength must be fos-
tered and decadence swept away.
(iv) There must be a thorough knowledge of present
world conditions, the international situation, and Japan's
position in the world.
and the absorption, without criticism, of western culture
must be checked.
(vi) Education of too intellectual a type must be
abandoned, stress must be laid upon moral training and im-
portance attached to practical education.
(vii) The physique of the nation must be improved.
(2) System in a conflict of ideas.
As a central organ in acnflict of ideas or in a propa-
ganda war, a national establishment such as a Ministry for
Propaganda, or an Intelligence Bureau, is, of course, im-
portant even in times of peace.
For

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.98)
- 37 -
For instance, the World War witnessed a conflict
of ideas, - now one of the methods of modern warfare-
which, under the name of propagania was conducted on a
large scale. The heroes in this propaganda war were Lord
Northcliffe of Great Britain, General Ludendorf of Ger-
many and President Wilson of thdUnited States of Ameri-
ca.
From the midale to the latter stages of the war, the
terrible force of propaganda showed its far-reaching ef-
fect from the rear of the fighting line in the enemy's
country and as fir as the principal towns and even in
the kitchens of the enemy; and Gernany at length collapsed
before it. This propaganca was of course carried on in con-
junction with military warfare an the economic blockade,
though one cannot overlook the f ct that warfare by propa-
ganda showed how important it was in itself.
Great Britain:
Immediately after the outbreak of the war, Great Bri-
tain extended the existing work of propaganda by establish-
ing a Press Bureau in August, 1914. An Intelligence Bu-
reau was separately established in January, l9l7, the work
of propaganda being centralised an actively taken up. La-
ter, an advisory committee, consisting of Lord Northcliffe
and three others, was organised and Lord Northcliffe pro-
ceeded to the United States on an important mission con-
nected with policy and propaganda and was very active in
that country. A Ministry of Intelligence was established
1n

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
- 38 -
in February, 1918, and Lord Beaverbrook assumed the port-
folio, while Lord Northcliffe was appointed as head of
the Section of Propaganda in the Enemy Countries. Later
there were further changes and Lord Northcliffe was made
Chairman of a Propaganda Policy Comaittee.
United States of America:
After the United States had entered the World War,
a Public Information Comnittee was org nised by President
Wilson in April, 19l7. The Committee was composed of the
Secretaries of State, War and Marine and Mr. George Creel.
The Committee was presided over by Ar. Creel and had com-
plete control over the work of propaganda at home an a-
broad.
France:
In France the Ministries of Foreign Affairs, War and
Marine each had its own propaganda section and carried on
propaganda in cooperation one with the other.
Germany:
Propaganda during the war in Germany was at first un-
controlled and limited tcthe use of one journal only; but
after much controversy botween the military and the Minis-
tries, a propaganda organisation was at length effect-
ed, - as strongly advocated by General Ludendorf - in Au-
gust, l9l8. It was, however, too late; for, as a result
of intense propaganda on the part of the allied Powers,
the war ended in Germany's failure.
It is greatly to be regretted that in Japan even in-
telligent and well-informed people have an insufficient
conception of the nature of a conflict of ideas. But, in
consequence

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.99)
- 39 -
consequence of the systematic org nisation of "red" pro-
paganda in Soviet Russia, how great is the anxiety felt
by all classes of th people in Japan! Owing to the in-
sufficiency of propaganda organs throughout the Manchurian
incident, what bitterness Japan has experienced! And in
the present trade and economic conflict, in what an un-
favorable position has Japan been placed by the propaganda
war carried on by the Powers! There is therefore no room
for any discussion as to the importance, in time of peace
and in time of war, of having a system in a conflict of
ideas.
4.
National def nce and military strength.
Negative preparedness; positive pre-
paredness; Soviet ano Japanese prepared-
ness; air strength an the urgent need
for extending civilian aviation.
Preparedness is the main factor in military warfare,
and may be sub-divided as follows: the minimum egree of
military strength necessary for the attainment of national
defence negatively and the strength required to attain
such object positiveIy. The former is determined automa-
tically by the national policy, the extent of (a country's)
territory, geographical position, etc.; the latter varies
in accordance with international conditions.
The military preparedness of the Japanese army is at
the moment the minimum required for negative national de-
fence as described above. Immediately after the great
War, when Soviet preparedness was inferior, it was thus
possible to attain passively the maintenance of peace in
Eastern Asia. But owing to the extension of the first

National Defonce and economics.
Economic adjustment; defects of exist-

(1)
Economic Adjustment: Defects of existing
machinery.
It must be admitted that the existing economic machinery
has contributed greatly towaris the economic development
of Japan. But from a national point of view, and more es-
pecially from that of national defence, there is still room
for improvement and adjustment on the following lines:
(1)

=== Page 247 === (confidence 0.99)
- 43 -
(1) The existing economic organisation has developed
on a basis of individualism and conversely there has been
a tendeney to leave econ mic activities to the interests
and wishes of individuals. Such activities, therefore,
have not accorded with the interests of the country and
the nation at large.
(ii) As th- result of severe competition, there is
a danger of exclusivism
a in class rivalries being aroused.
(1ii) Uneven istribution of wealth has led to poverty
and unemp.loyment for many of the people an: to a decline
of the smaller producers and agricultural population, and
there is little hope for stability of livelihood among the
people.
(iv) Owing to the lac: of national control the pre-
sent economic organisation is not adapted to the mobilisa-
tion anc centralisei use of the whole of our faculties in
the development of resources, in the encouragement of in-
dustry, and in the promotion of trade; while as a result of
the very restricted national Budget, even such equipment
as is absolutely essential to national ef nce connot be
secured.
Different opinions are held in regard to measures where-
by changes and improvements may be made in the present
economic organisation; but fro: the point of view of national
defence the following may be cited:-
(1) Advancement of the national development and the
welfare of the whole nation on a basis of an ethical view
of

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.98)
- 44 -
of economics in accordance with the ideals upon which
the country is founded.
(ii) Promotion of the ctivitits of th whole people
so that they shall have com ensation for their industry,
thereby bringing stability of livclihooc to the nation at
large.
(iii) Impr vement of th various monetary institutions
and of the conduct of in us'rial operations with a view to
the satisfactory exploitation of resources, encouragement
of inuustry, promotion of trace and replenishment of defen-
sive arrangements.
(iv) The steady encour gement or in ustry so as to
give satisfaction to in ivi ual orilinality and the desire
for work so long as they o not conflict with the needs of
the State.
(s) Establishment of a war-time economy.
As shown above, economic wurfarc has already begun even
under peace conditions. It is, of course, most severe when
it occurs in timos of war side by side with military warfare,
an the problem of economic control under such circumstances
is of vital importance from the point of viev of national
defence.
In all wars up to the beginning of the 20th century,
even where the belligerents strained every effort, there was
comparatively little demand for fighting military strength
and munitions of war, while war brought about little change
in the general economy of the nations. The World War, how-
ever, differed a great deal from the wars preceding it inas-
much as there was an unprecedented increase in the demand for
munitions

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.98)
- 45 -
munitions of war.
The belligernts' tracc an' con unicnt'ons with
the rest of the worl  we seri usly at  et d, an in some
cases ther was a state of blocka e. As a rosult the import
not only of military supplies but cv n of the daily requi-
sites of the people was stopped, while export trade ceased
and the links of peace-time world cconory vere severed.
Preparations must therefore be made in peacc-time for a suf-
ficiency, when recuired, of tnose resources of wh ch 
shortage in time of war is likely, the deman , at a time of
emergency, for an enormous amount of war munitions must be
met, while in order to maintain the ivelihooc of the peo-
ple there must be thorough control, mor especially of
defensive pro uction, trans ort an con unications an of
the economic life of th nation. Thi: wo result in many
changes in the economic organisation.
To take an example fron the World War, Germany, for
instance, which was blockaded by the Powers, experienced
extraordinary cifficulties in consequence of the cessation
of imports of provisions, arms and ammunition. Moreover
she lost her market for the sale of her surplus goods, and
every-one knows the economic straits to which she was re-
duced.
Again, although Great Britain maintained close touch
with the economic world in spite of the menace of German
submarines, she also experienced a shortage of sugar, wheat
and meat. As the result of the difficulty of importing raw
cotton, the cotton industry in Lancashire had to be abandoned,
while other fatal blows, which we have no leisure to enumerate,
were

=== Page 250 === (confidence 0.99)
- 46 -
were suffered by the national economy. The countries at war,
th r fore, ha to adopt an intensive po icy of protection
and encouragement as regar's their industry an imp rts
in order to make good a shortage of resources and provi-
sions, and some of them took over partial control. Even
in Great Britain, whose motto is absolute freedom, there
was compulsory cultivation of arable land, and irect State
control of flour mills, as well as of imnorts of wheat,
sugar and meat and the distribution thereof, while, in
order to remedy the acute situation in the cotton industry
in Lancashire, the Pritish Government established absolute
control over thc fair istribution of stocks of raw cotton
and took chirge of the problem of the adjustment of working
hours an the relief of unemployment. Moreover, while the
war lasted, the British Governnent also intervene as regards
all articles consumed by the people, almost ithout excep-
tion, inclu ing provisions, such as bread, meat and sugar,
as well as fuel anl clothing, by fixing a standard daily
amount to be used, and by istributing them by means of a
coupon system. Appropriate steps were also taken by the
Government for the relief of people affected by the war,
persons whose capital was in certain special industries
and laborers; while measures in regar? to persons who had
lost their occupation as a result of the war had also to
be devised.
The experience of the World War shows how important
in a war of the future will be preparedness for a war economy.
A country which is unprepared in this respect would at
once

=== Page 251 === (confidence 0.98)
- 47 -
once be exposed to great difficulties, whic: might well
culminate in loss of the war. Preparation in peace-time
must, therefore, be mate by Govermtent an people alike.
Items in regar to wh ch prepar tion is important
re:- Encouragenent of entcrprises relate to resources
of which there is likely to be a shortage in time of war;

ing resources fron foreign countries in time of war; prac-
tical promotion of de ensive inlustries for the utilisation
of such resources in peac time; an plans for the export
of surplus produets, for fin: rcc and monetary circulation
in time of war, for foreign trace and for labor, etc., etc.
Investig tions ane preparations on a large seale must be
made and a controlled war economy brought into operation
without delay as soon as war broaks out.
5.
Preparedness of the Nation.
We have here merely set Jown a few matters to which,
it is generally thought, it is impo tant to give effect at
once by way of a plan for national ef nce. Nat onal de-
fence is, of course, closely connected with the national
policy in general so f r as the growth and development of
the State are concerned, and many points requiring considera-
tion are not therefore touched upon in this pamphlet.
The Empire is making rapid strides; but, on the other
hand, heavy pressure from other Powers is constantly increas-
ing. It is the duty of thc Japanese people of the Showa era
to rise superior to the country's unprecedented difficulties -
which in fact amount to a trial as to whether the Empire is
to

=== Page 252 === (confidence 0.97)
-48 -
continue for ever to prosper or not - and to ad a
glorious page to her brilliant history of three thousand
years. May the whole nat'o  alise t'i meaning of national
defence; may they create an bring into use an organisation
centered in national defence; nd may they succeed in over-
coming their canger ther by advancing the spread of the
lofty ideals of th spirit of Japan and establishing a
lasting world peace.

Kobe's despatch No. 41l of December 8,
1934.
This despatch transmits, and describes
a Japanese book entitled "Cur Army and Navy"
written by a well known natriotic writer.
This publication is of a type very
common in Japan at present. It is prepa-
ganda for the armed forces, and is intended
to arouse patriotic ardor and enthusiasm
for war. It contains a good many references
to America as a potential enemy, and warns
against American imperialistic designs in
the Orient.
The most significant parts of the book
are the four introductions, by General
Hayashi, Admiral sumi, General Araki and
Admiral Okada. These introductions are
brief and simply commend the book to the
recder.
WIT.
WTT/DLY
894.20/128

book entitled "Warera no rik-kai-gun" ("Japan's Army and
Navy"), published by the Dai Nippon Yubenkai Koensha,
whose address is 3/19 Otowa-cho, Koishikawa-ku, Tokyo.
The author is Hirata Shinsaku, apparently quite a well
known patriotic author. One of his books, "Warera moshi
tatakawaba" ("If Japan Were To Fight") is advertised on
the paper jacket. The book has four introductions, by
F
General Hayashi, Admiral Osumi, General Araki, and Admiral
JAN17
B
Okada, the latter written evidently in bis capacity as
Admiral before he became Premier.
1935
The understaffed condition of this office with respect
to clerks and translators has made it possible to translate
only a small portion of this book. The following excerpts
JAN1719
were prepared by the wife of a resident American familiar
JAN 1 9 1935
with the Japanese language and are quoted verbatim:
FILED
Pages 41 to 43.
17193
"The
C of  
00122-30
dooe 
Tas.r.
returred o DOR

=== Page 266 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
"The Waves are running High in the Pacific.
"The Pacific is our sea. It is our mother, and
from now on the world will turn from the Atlantic era
to the Pacific.
East and West, ten thousand miles, South and North
three thousand miles of sea are beckoning. Now more than
over it is time for men of the maritime country to ride
forth. Listening carefully one hears the far sounds of
the Japan current like the barking of whales echoing
through. "Japan people of the sea, come. The Pacific
is yours." It seems to invite us; but Japan cannot
blindly go out on these seas. East and West the great
fleets of foreign countries are steadily glaring at us.
Particularly the American fleet which for thirty years
past has been thinking thusly: "We must find a way to
manage the Pacific." Even now with the world's largest
fleet in this ocean, they are boasting that "this is an
invincible fleet."
If one stands at the point of Izu bosaki and gazes
at the Eastern waterline, the waters of the Pacific extend
endlessly.
Four thousand five hundred miles distant is the
fortress at the golden gate of San Francisco. Three
thousand five hundred miles to the other side is the
fort at Hawaii, the land where palm trees grow. There
are 300 or more warships with their mas's assembled, at
anchor.
They are the king of the eastern Pacific and our
Navy is the bulwark of the western Pacific.
The world's largest fleet and the world's strongest
fleet are divided to the east and west, quietly facing
each other.
Between these two great fleets the Japan current flows.
The whale is barking. The waves are not quiet. We cannot
for a day look away from the sea."
Pages 43 to 44.
"The Pacific and the American Navy.
"out of the two thousand ships of the U.S. Navy,
150 are in the Atlantic. Why is the Navy away from the
Atlantic and gathered in the Pacific? (During the Spring
and until the Autumn of the ninth year of Showa they were
in the Atlantic but that was only for half a year.
About thirty years ago, the greatest of American
Naval strategists, Mahan, said: "From henceforward,
America must expand from the Atlantic to the Orient."
Mahan had a good mind. He thought of the Atlantic as an
old sea and of the Pacific as still in its youth, it was
a sea for youths. The country that sets forth on this
sea

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
sea will be successful, he thought. Therefore, the
Uni ted States looking forward ten and twenty years
or even a hundred, is mustering a great fleet in the
Pacific and is striving to become the king of this
sea and is by those means endeavoring to continue
flourishing as the first in the world.
Those ideas are truly stupendous, and the Navy
has for the past twenty years been planning out battles
(stratogies) for the time of the Pacific war.
Again, in Panama, Hawaii and San Francisco they
have fortresses with tens of thousands of cannon, and
they have built great bases so that their fleet may
invade the far ends of the Pacific.
In the North they have Alaska, the Aleutian
Islands, and such stepping stone bases, and in the
South, the Philippine Islands near our Formosa, and
the military base of Manila. To the East of that Guam,
a military base; all stepping stones toward the Orient.
It is as if the U.S. were embracing the Orient with
steel arms. Their large Navy is not only for the
purpose of protecting their own country. When the
time comes, they are ready to ride forth on the great
seas for thousands of miles for the purpose of invasion.
(Gentlemen and Ladies) you have probably heard of
the word "circling strategy". That is the name given
to their tactics when the American Navy goes out to
attaok distant places. This was thought out by Hughes
and Pratt. It is tactics involving the use of a
circular shape. The U. S. has 15 battleships. These
fifteen battleships and numerous destroyers surround
and make a ring and when the opposing battleships or
submarines come to attack they stop them with this
destroyer ring; but as that is insufficient, they send
out cruisers to guard them. It is certainly a good
precaution. The Admiral of the American Fleet, Sellers,
boasted: "We will not be elermed from whatever angle
our battle array is attacked."
Although we do not know how weak or how strong
the battle array is, when we think of their large Navy
and numerous bases coupled with their stupendous idea
to become king of the Pacific, Japan cannot be
inoautious."
Pages 45 to 46.
"Guard the Sea of Okhotsk to the Equator.
for distant invasions, our Navy is only thinking of
protecting the Orient. In the world there are some
people with the mistaken idea that perhaps Japan is
planning an invasion. Suoh people do not know the
honorable soul of the Navy.  The Emperor's Navy is
not an aggressive fleet. It is a nameless force.
It

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
It is not a country that starts unrighteous wars.
Our Army and Navy merely uphold and fulfill our
mission, and taking all responsibilities within
their two arms, are strongly protecting the Orient.
Therefore unless foreign navies invade us, the waves
of the Pacific will be calm forever; but when some
evil person comes forth to shatter the peace of the
Pacific, our two hundred floating castles will blow
steel fire and fight.
There is something we must stop to consider.
Our Navy must protect thousands of miles of seas to
the North, South, East and West. They have to protect
the North where the sea lions bark down to the tropical
South where blue corals float. In the East the Marshall
Islands 17o0, to the West as far as the ends of the
typhoon ridden Southern Chinese waters.
Western Pacific, Southern Chinese waters, Eastern
Chinese waters, Yellow Sea, Japan Sea, Sea of Okhotsk,
in order to protect these six seas, a small Navy will
not do. We must not have an inferior fighting force
to either England or the U.S. We must face the world
and show our power by clearly stating that "Japan will
protect the peace of the Orient."
Therefore, if numerically our fighting forces
should be a little inferior, our real fighting force
positively must never be inferior to anyone. Japan
is now one of the great countries of the world, and
it is the stronghold of the Orient. This responsibility
is heavy, the mission large.
If the strength of the Japanese Navy should at
all be lessened, the Orient will be managed by Navies
of foreign countries, and the independence of the
Orient will be destroyed. We must make a firm resolve
and finally make our Navy a strong one."
Pagos 46 to 47.
"Japan Current and the Equator are the Country's
Border Line.
"The navies that float naval flags in the South
Seas are, beside the Japanese and American; the
British, Chinese, Dutch, Soviet, French, Italian,
Siamese and the newly born Manchurian state. The
largest among these is the British. In Hongkong,
west of Formosa and Singapore in the South Seas and
the Malyan peninsula they have bases and are glaring
around at the Pacific, the China and Indian Seas.
The British China forces have assembled the
newest and most powerful cruisers in the British Navy
as well as air plane carriers, and as can be expected
of one of the oldest and greatest Navies, the white
ensign floats majestically.
The

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
The American Captain "Tenka"(?) said: "The
English Navy cannot fight the Japanese Navy in the
Far East; if they do, it will be a great and
desperate battle." While the greatest man in the
British Navy, Admiral Beatty said: "Aggressive war
in Eastern waters would be hopeless." and is sighing
with relief; but, as old as they are, they are the
King lion of the sea. We must think of their tusks
as still being strong.
Again in the South seas there is the Dutch Navy.
They are also people of the sea. Their Navy is small
but they have powerful destroyers besides having ten
submarines. They have strength which we cannot ignore.
China though weak has 70 war ships, while the
Soviet has a fleet of submarines hidden in Vladivostok.
At the moment, the oriental seas are not peaceful.
The American Admiral, Knox, praising the strength
of our Navy said: "The Japanese within the length of
many thousands of miles of defense have the authority
of a king." But we must not depend on such words and
relax our vigilance.
The world now knows the strength of the Japanese
Navy. Although our Navy is silent, the world is afraid
of that power and does not stretch out its hands toward
the Orient.
If the Japanese Navy retains this power, the
Pacific will be peaceful forever. Again, if our Navy
should lose their power, in a short time bloody winds
will blow and the wild waves will probably sing a war
song.
Even now, our glorious Navy looking upon the
Japan current and Equator as our borderline, 1s
forever playing guardian of the Pacific."
Pages 251 to 262 apparently contain references to
the American navy but it has not been possible to obtain
a translation of them.
It is not believed that this book is used as a
toxtbook in the public schools but it is understood that
at least one private middle sohool, and possibly others,
use similar books as part of their course of study.
Only one oopy of this book, which will be forwarded
to the Embassy at Tokyo for inspection and transmission to
the Department, has been obtainable to date. The Consulate
18

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.93)
-8-
is ondeavoring to obtain another copy which can be
forwarded to Washington or Tokyo as desired.
Respectfully yours,
Hmns Pinnon
Howard Donovan,
American Consul.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

February 15, 1935.
MON:

Peiping's despatch No. 3279 of
January 18, 1935, in regard to a
Japanese military conference at Dairen.
No action required.
The despatch s tates that a number
of important Japanese military officers
of the most reactionary type held a
conference at Dairen on January 4 and 5.
Lieutenant-Colonel Shibayama, a moderate,
was not present. According to a Japanese
press report the officers at the confer-
ence expressed strong dissatisfaction
with General Chiang Kai-shek and with
the failure of the Chinese to carry out
certain terms of the Tangku truce of
May 31, 1933. The despatch points out

may have been made to intimidate the
Chinese or it might have been a part of
a policy, advocated by at least a section
of the Japanese military, of weakening
the position of General Chiangin order
to effect a more rapid disintegration
of China.
The Japanese press includes in the
unfulfilled pledges of the Tangku truce
agreement aerial communication, direct

LEGATION OF THE
