Peiping, January 18, 1935.
Subject: Japanese Military Conference
at Dairen.
lie
894.20
mitt
2
793.94
FEJL
5
894.20/1
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
31
3
Sir:
I have the honor to report that a conference
FIL
of a number of important Japanese military officers
all
1
which was held on January 4 and 5 at Dairen, has
created some uneasiness in China because of the
character of the attending officers and their views
with regard to China as reported in the press.
These officers appear to be definitely of the
3
most reactionary type, including Majors-General
Doihara and Itagaki, both of whom are regarded as
having been leaders in the plotting and carrying
out
145-1

=== Page 281 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
out of the seizure of Manchuria, as well as Lieutenant-
1
Colonel Kagesa, Assistant Military Attache stationed
at Shanghai, who has made during recent months a
number of inflammatory statements expressing dis-
satisfaction with the attitude of China toward Japan.
The significance of a meeting of this character may
be the greater because of the fact that Lieutenant-
1
Colonel Shibayama, who holds relatively moderate
views and who at the time of the conference was still
Assistant Military Attache stationed at Peiping and
chief negotiator with the Chinese over problems
affecting North China, did not attend the confer-
ence, although it would seem that he normally ought
to have done so. Moreover, the conference was held
within ten days after the arrival at Hsinking of
General Minam1 (who was Minister of War at the time
of the Manchurian incident), for the purpose of
taking over the duties of Commander-in-Chief of the
Kwantung Army and Ambassador to Manchukuo".
According to the press (including a Japanese
newspaper published at Tientsin and subsidized by
the Japanese military), these officers expressed
strong dissatisfaction with General Chiang Kaishek
and with the failure of the Chinese to carry out
certain terms of the Tangku Truce of May 31, 1933.
This is by no means the first time that Japanese
military officers have expressed dissatisfaction
with regard to the "sincerity" of General Chiang Kai-
shek.
However, whether the expression of dis-
satisfaction
145-2

=== Page 282 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
satisfaction at this conference has any especial im-
portance, it is difficult to say. It may merely
have been made to intimidate the Chinese; or it might
have been a part of the policy advocated by at least
a section of the Japanese military, namely, a policy
of weakening the position of General Chiang Kai-shek
in order to effect a more rapid disintegration of
China.
With regard to the charge of Chinese failure to
carry out the pledges of the Tangku Truce, it was
understood that the principal promises had finally
been fulfilled, with the exception of the establish-
ment of aerial communication; but the Japanese press
itemizes the unfulfilled pledges, in addition to
aerial communication, as direct telegraph and tele-
phone service, through railway-freight service, and
a customs agreement. The questions of direct tele-
graph and telephone service and of through railway-
freight service do not appear to be of vital import-
ance. There exists, for example, telegraph service
between Peiping and Mukden by way of Chefoo while tele-
grams addressed to Harbin from Peiping go by radio
from Tientsin. The direct telephone service which
existed prior to the Manchurian incident was not
satisfactory. The establishment last July of through
passenger train service between Mukden and Peiping
would seem to reduce considerably the importance of
any question of a through freight service between
those two cities.
The
1453

=== Page 283 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
The reference to a customs agreement is difficult
to understand. Chinese customs houses were establish-
ed last summer along the Great Wall, and revenue is
being collected at them only on products which did
not originate in Manchuria. It is possible that the
Japanese military may refer to certain technical
questions, or to "a closer cooperation for the al-
leged purpose of making it unnecessary for travelers
to undergo two examinations, one at "Manchukuo
stations north of the Great Wall and one at Chinese
stations south of it. It is also possible that the
question of customs is related to some larger problem
of the Chinese customs tariff which, it is under-
stood, is now under revision. When the Chinese of-
ficial in charge of Chinese customs houses along the
Great Wall was questioned a few days ago in this
regard, he stated that he did not know to what the
Japanese military referred.
Respectfully yours,
F2e
X
C. E. Gauss,
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

February 25, 1935.
My dear Dr. Colegrove:
I refer to your letter, under date Fobruary 22, 19s5,
in which you ask whether the Department has a copy of the
894.20/134
pamphlet entitled, "Kokubo no Hongi to Sono Kyokwa no
Teisbo", published last September by the Japanese War
Department, or a translation thereof, and whether it would
be possible for the Department to lend you the pamphlet and
translation.
Thero is in the Department's archives a translation of
the pamphlet to which you refer, but in view of the fact
that the translation is an annex to, and therefore a part
of, an official report from the Embassy it would not be
possible under the rules of the Department to comply with
your request. The translation runs into forty-eight pages
of double-spaced foolscap, and I would not feel justified
in having so lengthy a document copied in the Department
for private distribution. However, I would be pleased to
make

Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
I am writing to inquire whether, in case the Department
has a translation of the Japanese Army Pamphlet issued on March
1935, it would be possible for me to have a copy of it made at my
expense by some one trusted by the Department.
894.207143
The name of the pamphlet is: "Hiji-ni Taisuru Warera
Kokumin-no" (We Must Prepare for the National Emergency), issued by
the Rikugun-sho Shimbun Han (Press Bureau of the War Department),
March 1935.
I appreciate the fact that there must be a limit to the
accommodation of the Department in such matters, and I will fully
understand your position if your answer to this request has to be
in the negative.
With high regards and esteem, I am
Faithfully yours,
Kenneth Cologeme

THE BARBAROUS WEST
Japan is in a better position than other
nations, because of its unrivaled culture.
to lead the crusade for human welfare.
the press section of the War Office tells
the public in its latest pamphlet. titled
The Determination of the Japanese Na-
tion at the Time of Crisis Tha. Marks the
30th Anniversary of the Russo-Japanese
War. of which about 200,000 copies were
ready yesterday for distribution to ex-
soldiers' organisations. schools. Young
Men's Associations. army regiments and
numerous social institutions.
Before Japan can embark on this
crusade in earnest, however, says the
pamphlet. it must set things right in its
own house. which means that it must
liquidate the evils brought about by in-
toxication with Western civilisation As
there are many countries that fail to
appreciate the aims of Japan. moreover.
external obstacles must also be overcome
As the War Minister. General Senjuro
Hayashi. was questioned in the Diet, on
the pamphlet published in October by
the press section of the War Office. which
aroused widespread criticism because of
its recommendations for social and poli-
tical reforms. says the Tokyo Jiji. special
care has been exercised in connection
with the new pamphlet to give no cause
for accusatlons against the army.
The publication is divided into five sec-
tions. The first is the foreword. followed
by a summary of the stages of Japan's
national development from the Meiji
Restoration, through the Russo-Japanese
War, the adoption of Western civilisation.
the World War. the period before the
Manchurian incident, in which individual-
ism flourished and national thought fluc-
tuated, and the present stage of transition
for realisation of the true Japan. The
third part treats the present international
situation. including the evolution of
modern thought. the advancement of
natural science, international issues and
changes in concepts of national defence.
The meaning of the present national crisis
 given in the fourth part. and the fifth
sets forth the path to be followed by the
nation.
PHASE OF UNREST
The Jiji gives the following summary of
the publication:
"The unrest that prevails throughout
the world is nothing but a phase through
which we must pass in the evolution of
human society. It creates an ordeal for
the Japanese to undergo as a stage in
their advancement. There are some who
contend that the crisis is only a figment
of the imagination and does not exist in
reality. Their contention is the argument
of persons who are blind to current world
affairs, a mere pretence of those too lazy
to make progress, or a deliberate fallacy
advanced by those who desire to main-
tain the status quo because of selfish
interests.
"The time has come when we must
liquidate modern civilisation that is based
on individualism. Unless this is done,
the feeling of unrest that prevails
throughout the world cannot be eased.

MANY CRISES OVERCOME

Each time it has surmounted its difficul-
ties and emerged with a higher status
in the world and greater welfare as a
nation. Whenever we have adopted a
foreign civilisation, moreover. we have

imported. Our present domestic troubles

REGARDING:

=== Page 314 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
(b)
Army and Navy.
(1)
Removal from Office of General Jinsaburo
Masaki as Inspector General of Military
Education in the Japanese Army and His
Replacoment by General Jotaro Watanabe.
On July 16 the War Office announced that General
Jinsaburo Masaki had been removed from his post of
Inspector General of Military Education in the Japanese
Army and had been replaced by Goneral Jotaro Watanabe.*
General Hayashi took this decision because of differences
with General Masaki rogarding forthcoming Army shifts
and promotions and also because of past disagreements
between the two men, both personal and political.
The KoKUMIN, a newspaper said to be subsidized by
the Japanese Army, stated on July ls that General Hayashi
had been impelled by two aims during his incumbency as
Minister of War, the unification of the Army, with the
establishment of stricter control over it, and the
assignment ef capable men in important positions. An
analysis of the list of August promotions and assignments,
unofficially released for publication by the War Office,
indicates that the conservative type of officer not
interested in political activities has been favored over
the adventurous and undisciplined type addicted to
political scheming. However, these shifts in the Japanese
Army have been made from within the Army and cannot be
said to be the result of coercion of the military by the
civilian element.**
The JAPAN TIMES of July 18 and the JAPAN ADVERTISER
of July 19 reported that at a conference of the Army
members
*
Embassy's telegram No. 151 8f July 16, 1935.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 14l4. July 26, 1935, and Embassy
telogram No. 151 of July 16, 1935.

=== Page 315 === (confidence 0.97)
一-
members of the Supreme Military Council held on July 17
had
there developed heated discussions on the question of
strengthening the contral control of the Army. The latter
newspaper reported that one of the members of the Council
had declared that though the dismissal of General Masaki
could not have been avoided it was an outrage to allow
the impression to prevail among the peopl. that General
Masaki, "who served the nation so faithfully during the
Manchurian crisis", was disturbing unity in the Army.
Another member was said to have expressed regret that
the removal of General Masaki had caused the rublic to
think that there were factions in the Army that could
not "see eye to eye", thus tending to weaken confidenc
in the military. According to the JAFAy AIVERTISER,
after four hours of discussion the members of the Council
finally agreed that everything possible should be done
to strengthen the control within the Army and to take
precautions against future occurrences similar to the
Masaki affair.
General Watanabe, the new Inspector General of
Military Education, was said by the Tokyo NICHI NICHI of
July 18 to have told a representative of that newspaper
on July 16 that he did not approve of participation by
the military in politics.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

September 10, 1935.
I have carefully reviewed Tokyo's
1450 of August 23, 1935, and I find that
it adds very little to the information
contained in Tokyo's telegram 166 of
August 13, 5 p.m., in which the fact of
the assassination was reported to the
Department.
Attention is drawn to the statement
on page 3 of the despatch -- "at the
present time there is no evidence to
indicate that General Hayashi will be
seriously embarrassed". During the last
few days General Hayashi has resigned,
indicating, as observed by the Embassy,
"that a policy of organizing the army
as a disciplined unit free from political
entanglements is a policy which has
obstacles to overcome".

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.95)
EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Tokyo, August 23, 1935.
No. 1450.
SUBJECT:
ASSASSINATION OF MAJOR GENERAL NAGATA.
3-46
1
894.20/145
The,Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
sir:
 In reference to the Embassy's telegram No. 166,
August 13, 1935, 5 p.m., on the subject of the assassina-
SEP 14 :035
tion of Major General Nagata, I have the honor to submit
the following report of the incident.
Major General Tetsuzan Nagata was the director of
NBFR
the military affairs bureau, and in that capacity was
the ranking bureau chief in the ministry of war. He
was at his desk promptly on the morning of August 12
when Lieutenant Colonel Saburo Aizawa entered the room
NBr
and
F/FG.

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
and inflicted severe knife wounds upon him. An officer
who was in the room at the time attempted without success
to defend Nagata. Aizawa was immediately taken and placed
under arrest, and his identity was withheld from the press
until the following day after he had been questioned by
the authorities. During the course of the day Nagata was
raised to the rank of lieutenant general and promoted also
in court rank. His death was announced ashaving occurred
late in the afternoon, but this was probably the usual
fiction which permits promotion before the official time
of death. It is likely that he died within a few minutes
of the attack.
No assassination of this importance has taken place
in the Japanese army in many years. The public was as-
tounded, and uneasiness was prevalent during the day that
the assassin's name was secretively withheld. Then came
the announcement of is identity, and as soon as the papers
informed the public that Aizawa was personally disgruntled
at his recent assignment to Taiwan, where he was to become
a military instructor in a school, the tension was immediate-
ly relieved. The press was notably unanimous in favoring
strong action against Aizawa for the sake of rigid army
discipline, and in general expressed itself for War Minister
Hayashi's continuing in office and continuing the policies
for which he stands.
The government succeeded in quieting the first fear
that

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
that Aizawa might be acting for a politically ambitious army
clique; but still the case is not closed. Several reports
have asserted vaguely that Aizawa was influenecd by a cer-
tain widespread opinion, insinuating that opposition to
some of the high officers is general. The newspapers have
reported long conferences on the incident between General
Hayashi and Prince Kanin, as well as with Generals Araki
and Masaki. It is probable that opponents of General Hayashi
are inclined to attach importance to the assassination as
expressing a general discontent in the army, but at the
present time the!e is no evidence to indicate that General
Hayashi will be seriously embarrassed by the sequel to the
incident. The subject of the Nagata assassination is to
be reviewed at a meeting of the military council in the
near future, until which time General Hayashi's success in
handling the matter as an isolated act of pe sonal dissatis-
faction on the part of the assassin can not positively be
stated. What the affair does indicate, even at its present
stage, is that a policy of organizing the army as a disci-
plined unit free from political entanglements is a policy
which has obstacles to overcome. It is not probable that
Nagata's assassination had any great significance; but it
is quite clear that a few men still remain in the army who
wish to make the most of the incident to embarrass the
existing administration.
Respectfully yours
Edwin L. Neville
X
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim
800.

Copy to Legation, Peiping.

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure
Translation
from
FRANKFURTER ZEITUNG of August 16,1935.
Why General Nagata Died.
.......There is no doubt that the O position in the Japan-
ese army pursued the same aims as its leaders - to increase
the power of the Empire to the outside and to revive old
Japanese tradition on the inside....
The oificers' movement to which Nagata's murderer ad-
hered is authoritarian, anticapitalistic, and nationalis-
tio. The officers of the Japanese army are sprung, for
the most part, from less wealthy circles. The descendants
of the old warrior caste al so are usually not rich. For
this reason, if for no other, they feel connected wi h the
plain soldiers, sons of peasants and laborers, who suffer
severely under the economic crisis and who were not able
to participate in Japan's economic revival like the big
bourgeois families who are in back of the political par-
ties in Parliament.....The domestic aim (of the Japanese
officers) is a "total mobilization," the subordination
of all individual activity to the demands of the state,
only because they see in this mental attitude the sole
possibility for Japan to gain her foodstuffs and liv-
ing basis in the next century......
HCF:EM

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DHSNRHEASTERNAFFAIRS
StP 1 Soptember 26, 1935.
DPPADTN
There is hereto appended a translation,
made by the Embassy at Tokyo, of a pamphlet
1ssued by the Japanese War Department on
September 5, 1935, on the subject of the
critical international situation. It will
be recalled that several other pamphlets
of a similar nature have been issued by the
Japanese army authorities on previous
occasions. These pamphlets are widely
distributed, and exert a considerable in-
fluence on Japanese public opinion. A
brief review of this latest pamphlet may
be of interest.
The chief aim of this pamphlet, as
was that of the others, appears to be to
keep the public aroused to dangers from
abroad and thereby to facilitate appropria-
tions for military purposes. Soviet Russia
is represented as the arch enemy. The
sale of the Chinese Eastern Railway, far
from evidencing a desire for peace on the
part of Soviet Russia, merely means that
Soviet Russia has retired temporarily
from an exposed position in order to con-
solidate her strength, to which end she
is utilizing payments from the sale of the
railway.
The

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
The United States is pictured as
second only to Soviet Russia in point of
danger to Japan. As evidence of America's
nofarious ambitions in the Orient, the
United States attempted to wrest part of
the fruit of Japan's victory after the
Russo-Japanese War through the Harriman
scheme in Manchuria. Again, the Washing-
ton Conference was designed to check
Japanese development on the continent.
At the time of the Shanghai incident
Mr. Stimson would have declared war on
Japan except for the warning of Admiral
Pratt that the American navy was inferior
to the Japanese navy. The United States
is now actively engaged in rebuilding her
navy, having learned a lesson from the
Shanghai incident, and when supremacy has
been attained Japan may expect further
pressure against her continental policy.
The boldest and most noteworthy
feature of the pamphlet is its insistence
upon a redistribution of the world's
territory and natural resources in order
to provide a lasting foundation for inter-
national peace. Population is advocated
as the basis for this distribution, and
the only mention of a need for limiting
population is curtly dismissed as
fanatical.
WT
WTT/DLY
—mm.N

newspapers state that one hundred thousand copies were
printed and are being distributed.
In spite of a large quantity of windy repetitions
the chief contentions of the pamphlet are easily dis-
cernible as in the main the same as in earlier army
pamphlets. Soviet Russia is represented as the arch
enemy, with the United States in sec nd place. China's
only true friend is Japan. Japan's great mission is to
establish universal peace.
The boldest and most noteworthy feature of the
tract of September 5 is its insistence upon a redistri-
bution of the world's territory and natural resources
to establish a lasting foundation for international
justice. Population is advocated as the basis of this
redistribution, and the only mention of a need for limiting
population is curtly dismissed as fanatical.
It is too soon to judge how the tract is being re-
ceived by the public. It was released only two days ago.
The Embassy's translation has been made rapialy, and
any important errors which may later be discovered will
be reported.
Respectfully yours,
am herill
Edwin L. Neville
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

No.1467 of sept 7, 1935- from the

Translation by the Americen Embassy, Tokyo,
of a pamphlet in Japanese received on September
5, 1935.
h:g
Japan and the Period of Change in the
International Situation.
War Department Newspeper Section, September 1935.
1. How is the intern tional situation evolving?
2. Two Tendencies in the world's peace thinking.
3. Is Japan's danger past?
4. The new situation in eastern Asia.
5. True peace and the way for Japan.

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.99)
1. How is the international situation evolving?
All things forever change. Thoughts and ideas differ.
States rise and fall. The history of mankind is a
history of the unceasing rise and fall of nations. In
this cycle there are periods of slow change and periods
of rapid change.
The world today is undergoing an evolution in
thoughts and in economic and political ideas.
As a result of the world war tl re has come a period
of adjustment of past cultures though the majority of man-
kind is now eagerly bent upon maintaining outworn ideas.
The principles of freedom which previously pervaded
the entire world are dying out. The outbreak of the world
war was due to a contest of imperialism which was the
ultimate outgrowth of this principle of free enterprise.
The freedom of the past must be corrected.
In the world there is an abundance of natural re-
sources awaiting development. In the past, when there was
room for free activity by individuals and states, un-
restricted enterprise was the keynote.
What is the condition today? In every country economic
life is in a wretched state. Viewed from the international
standpoint, practically all the world's resources have
been appropriated by the various powers acting imperialisti-
cally. The League of Nations was established with the idea
of improving this situation. Subsequently the Washington
and London naval treaties and the pact against war were
entered into with the objeot of maintaining world peace.
These peace organs were effective to a certain ertent
in the period following the war. But the ever-changing
world

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
world is not to be thus orystalised in its condition
at a given moment,and Japan's bold leap and Germany's
sudden outburst followed. The world today is in a
state of uneasiness because of the conflioting efforts
of the powers. With populations increasing in the face
of limited resources and an excess of goods for limited
markets, a clash seems inevitable between free enterprise
and government control, between individualism and collecti-
vism. An extraordinary state of affairs exists the world
over.
Various countries are now forming economic blocks
against others, thereby bringing about political and
diplomatic disputes. In this situation Japen is doing
its utmost to bring about a real world peace under the
principles of brotherhood.
2.
Two Tendencies in the world's peace thinking.
Generally speaking two principles stand out in con-
tradiction to each othor. One is imperialism and the
other is the idea of internationalism now prevalent
among small countries suffering from restricted natural
resources.
This difference of idea is the cause of uneasiness
among the nations todey. Naturally the question arises
as to what plan should be adopted in adjusting them.
It is hardly necessary to point out that a few decades
ago, when Japan awoke from its seclusion, she found that
nearly all natural resources in the world, and especially
in the Far East, had already been appropriated by the
European powers and the United States.
The Sino-Japanese war, won by Japan, afforded Japan
an opportunity to make an advence upon the Asiatic continent.
It

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
It is still fresh in our memory thet immediately after
the close of the Sino-Japanese war pressure was brought
to bear upon Japan by France, Germany, and Russia.
In the Russo-Japanese war Japan gained a victory
and as a result secured various rights and interests in
Manchuria. The United States launched a movement to
participate in these rights and interests. The United
States planned to purchase the South Manchuria Railway
through the medium of Mr. Harriman, to establish a bank
of Manchuria with the backing of American capital, to
construct the Chinchou-Aigun railway, and to neutralize
the South Manchuria Railway. Fortunately these Americen
ambitions were not realized.
In the world war Jepen, with the aid of the Anglo-
Japenese alliance, drove German influence from Shantung
province in China. Shortly afterwards the Washington
conference was opened and as a result Shantung province
was returned to China. The Ishii-Lansing agreement was
then oaneeled. By the conclusion of the Nine Power
Treaty Japan's policy on the Asiatic continent was ob-
structed. Following the collapse of the old Russian
Empire the Anglo-Japanese Alliance was abrogated. Further-
more by the Washington and London naval treaties a reduc-
tion in the strength of the Japanese navy was attempted.
Since the Meiji Restoration of 1868 up to the present,
verious powers have brought pressure to bear upon Japan on
account of her growing international position and increas-
ing influence in eastern Asia. Since the outbreak of the
Manchurian incident certain states have joined to bring
pressure upon Japan. It is therefore fairly certain that

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
if Japan should be successful in establishing a union
between China, Manchukuo, and herself for the sake of
peace in the Far East, further pressue will be brought
to bear upon Japan by the European powers and the
United States because of the fear that their economio
policies in the Far East may be jeopardized. Herein
lies a serious national danger for Japan in the future.
An Americen named Simonds has published a book entitled
"The Price of Peace" in which he suggests that the
powers of the world should be divided into two groups,
namely, the countries which are satisfied and the
countries which are not satisfied. If the former should
supply the latter with necessary raw materials and make
concessions in favor of those countries which are suffer-
ing from over-population there would be no war. He fur-
ther remarks that the United States possesses rich natural
resources and vast territory, that the United States never-
theless is stretching its hands for other territories, and
that such a policy is not fair. This remark is really
worthy of consideration. If the various powers should be
magnanimous enough to give natural resources and vast
overseas territories to other countries which are suffering
from lack of natural resources the whole of mankind would
enjoy mutual prosperity and happiness. If this were done
world peace would be brought about. Otherwise a collision
between the two groups above mentioned is unavoidable. Look
at Germany today. Under the treaty of Versailles she has
suffered for fifteen years. Now Germany has seceded from
the League of Nations and has declared that she will build
up

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
up her military armament, to the great surprise of the
entire world. The cause of discontent among all nations
today is the unfair distribution of territories and
natural resources the world over.
An authority on the population question holds that
the history of nations is an eternal struggle for the
acquisition of land and foodstuffs; and Simonds' thesis
is thst present world uneasiness is due to eoonomic
discontent rather than political discontent.
Japan today is now facing a serious orisis because
she is suffering from lack of natural resources and re-
stricted territories as well as from the pressure brought
to bear by the various powers economically. A few decades
ago when Japan awoke from her seclusion she was far below
the level of the United States and the European powers in
every respect. As a result of her struggle Japan today
is keeping abreast.
The Manchurian incident has afforded Japan a unique
opportunity to expand in all directions. In foreign trade,
in cotton goods, rayon, and other miscellaneous artioles,
Japan is now penetrating into all parts of the world. It
is therefore not to be wondered at that some European
countries criticize Japan as a destroyer of world peace.
Some assert that Japan's commercial advance is made possible
by dumping. Such criticism is subsiding because of the
realization that Japan's successful commerce results from
her cheap labor and the exchange rate.
America and Europe have rich resources and abundant
territories and should give Japan either sufficient lands
and

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
and resources or allow Japanese immigration. Professor
Cannon of the University of London, in his work entitled
"Wealth", remarks that such nations as do not practice
birth control should not be allowed the right of emigrat-
ing. Such a statement is fanatical.
The independent countries of the Far East are in
possession of territories covering thirteen per cent of
the world. The population question requires international
settlement. Principal items for discussion at future
international conferences should be fair distribution
of population, international control of raw material and
foodstuffs, and international erohange of industrial pro-
duction, thereby bringing about ideal world peace.
3.
Is Japan's danger past?
Already four years have elapsed since the occurrence
of the Manchurian inoident. During this interval Japen
has successfully faced a national crisis more than once.
Three years have elapsed since the establishment of Manchukuo.
As a result of perfect harmony and concord between Japan
and Manchukuo the latter is now attaining healthy develop-
ment. This is a matter of congratulation. However, there
are some who say that the critical days are now over. Such
a view is fundamentally unsound. Other sections of the
Japanese people express the opinion thet it is unfortunate
that the so-called crisis of l935-36 has not materialized;
that, on the contrary, as a result of the transfer of the
North Manohuria Railway tension between Japen and Soviet
Russia has been alleviated; that the United States seems
to

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
to be rofraining from any positive policy regarding
the Far Eastern question; that European powers and the
League of Nations are now too busy in dealing with the
Italo-Abyssinian question to turn their attention to the
Far East; and that China today realizes the necessity
of cooperating with Japan and of shelving the Manchurian
question. In view of ell this, in their opinion, the
present is a unique opportunity for Japan to reduce
military expenditure and thereby to save her strength for
other development.
Quite recently Soviet Russia has concluded anti-war
treaties with various governments, joined the League of
Nations, and transferred the North Manchurian Railway
to Japan and Manchukuo. As a consequence Soviet Russia
is now str gthening her military forces in the Far East.
It is also an astonishing fact that the United States
wishes to enforce upon Japan an inferior naval ratio
of sirty per cent. The United States is actively engaged
in a program of strengthening her navy up to 1939.
If the Japenese nation should lose its zeal for the
consummation of the three great policies, namely, coopera-
tion between Japan and China, development of Mrnohukuo as
an indopendent state, and the building up of military
strength, she will be unable to maintain peace in the
Far East and will also encounter a serious national crisis
in the future. This is the reason why we ask, in the pre-
sent pamphlet, in what direction is the world moving today.
There are some who assert that the Manohurian incident is
now closed, but such a view is totally mistaken. A funda-
mental cause of the Manchurian incident was the anti-
Japanese

=== Page 334 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8-
Japanese movements in China whereby Japan's rights and
interests were trampled under foot. Unless this funda-
mental cause is removed the Manchurian incident cannot
be regarded as settled. China today relies upon the
United States and the European powers, as in the past,
and continues anti-Japanese agitation and plots to dis-
turb Manchukuo. Relations between Japan and China cannot
be said to have been restored to their former status. The
North China situation has recently occurred and still re-
mains unsettled. It was brought about by China's scheming
to disturb Manchukuo and comnitting various offenoes
against the Japanese authorities in North China.
Manchukuo is now strengthening her borders against
Soviet Russia in the north. In the west China is always
watching an opportunity to start anti-Manchukuo movements.
Japan is meantime determined to protect Manchukuo against
foreign aggression.
Up to the present no power has ever attacked Japan.
This is because of Japan's location. During the Manchurian
incident the former Secretary of State Stimson favored
opening hostilities against Japan but his plan was not
oarried out. This was due to the fact that Japan's navy
was powerful and that America's navy had not reaohed the
limit granted by the London naval treaty.
Soviet Russia under the first and second five year plans
intends to control the world. If the Japanese people should
think that the Manohurian inoident has already olosed and
that the oritical times have disappeared, it is quite ap-
parent that they will face disaster.
4

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
4. The new situation in eastern Asia.
a. Relations between Japen and Soviet Russia.
Viewed fron the standpoint of Soviet Russia the trans-
fer of the North Manchuria Railway is apparently an expres-
sion of peace. Insofar as Manchukuo is concerned, this
transfer may be regarded as a temporary abandonment of a
positive polioy on the part of Soviet Russia in the Far
East. By this transfer the principal cause of dispute
botween Manchukuo and Soviet Russia is removed. Viewed
from other standpoints, however, if a war should occur
between Japan and Soviet Russia it would be wellnigh im-
possible for Soviet Russia to maintain control of the
North Manohuria Railway. This is the reason why Russia
sold the railway. With the money accruing from the sale
of the North Manchuria Railway Soviet Russia is now try-
ing to push forward its five year plan, so she may be said
to have killed two birds with one stone. Speaking candidly,
the transfer of the North Manchuria Railway was not due
to eny desire of Soviet Russia for the maintenance of peace.
It was motivated by practical considerations. It is there-
fore a great mistake to suppose that the principal causes
for future disputes between Japan and Soviet Russia have
been removed. The maintenance by Russia of a powerful
army in the Far East is a source of future disputes be-
tween Japan and Manchukuo and Soviet Russia.
In Manchukuo Japan maintains an army sufficient to
guard the railway and preserve peace and order. On the
contrary Soviet Russia has an army of 230,000 men, 11 divisions
of infantry and three divisions of cavalry, east of Baikal.
Russia

=== Page 336 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
Russia is thus in a position to attack Manchukuo at any
moment. Alcng the frontier boundary line the Soviet
Union has constructed forts which are of permanent
character and is now building barracks at various im-
portant points. Before the occurrence of the Manchurian
inoident she had only 80 airplanes in the Far East, but
in the latter part of last year Soviet planes in the Far
East numbered about 6o0 and at present their number has
increased to 750. Bofore the incident Russia had only
l00 military tanks and now has 650. In addition Soviet
Russie has a number of airplanes which are able to attack
Tokyo, Osaka, and other districts. At Vladivostok she
has 50 submarines. If a war should occur between Japan
and Soviet Russia there is a danger that Japen's communi-
cation with the continent might be cut off. The presence
of a strong Soviet army in the Far East is indeed a serious
question for Japan from a national standpoint. Some say
that in view of the situation in Europe, particularly in
Germany, Soviet Russia will transfer its army in the Far
East to western Europe. But at the present time Japan
cannot disoern that Soviet Russia has any such intention.
At a Soviet congress held in January this year the Soviet
authorities emphasized the importance of maintaining a
powerful army in the Far East. From this it is quite olear
that Soviet Russia has no intention whatever either to re-
duce or to withdraw her Far East military foroes. The
Soviet Vice Minister of National Defense recently announced
that it is necessary for a large country like Russia to
provide strong military forces in the west and the east
to

=== Page 337 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
to provide against future emergencies. He further re-
marked that the position of Soviet Russia differs from
that of Germany which may transfer its armies to the
west and east very quickly and easily.
A noteworthy fact is that emong arms carried by
bandits in Manchukuo are many rifles, machine guns,
and ammunition made in Soviet Russia. At the time the
North Manchuria Railway was transferred to Japan some
suggested the conclusion of a non-aggression treaty be-
tween Japan and Soviet Russia. But so long as Soviet
Russia maintains a powerful army in the Far East, which
is a meance to Japen, the conclusion of such a non-ag-
gression treaty would be meeningless.
Between Japen, Manchukuo, and Soviet Russia there
are various questions pending, such as the fishery question,
oil question in Russisn Saghalien, boundary question be-
tween Manchukuo and Soviet Russia, and instigation by
Soviet Russia of bandits to disturb the peace of Manchukuo.
Despite the fact that Soviet Russia transferred the North
Manchuria Railway to Japan she is hurriedly oonstructing
railways in Siberia to provide for future emergencies.
Under Soviet Russia's five year plan she is now planning
to inaugurate electric power stations in distriots west
of Baikal and also planning to establish an area of heavy
industry near certain rich coal mines in Siberia. At
Komsomorsk arms and munitions factories, airfields and
chemical factories have already been established. At
Irkutsk munitions factories are being established. Soviet
military

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
-12 -
military forces in the Far East are thus able to obtain
a supply of neoessary materials through two great rail-
ways. At Volga and Ural the Soviet Union is now basing
a number of army divisions which in time of emergenoy
will be sent to the Far East. This is worthy of at-
tention. The second five yeer plan of Russia will be
completed by 1937. At the present nothing is known as
to whether Russia will launch another five year plan.
Everyone is well aware that Soviet Russia is now
stretching its magic hand toward outer Mongolia, Central
China, and other regions. The day is not far distant
when outer Mongolia will be converted into Russien
territory. In view of all this the relations between
Japan, Manchukuo, and Soviet Russia arc becoming ever
more complicated. So long as Soviet Russia will not
abandon its propaganda to communize the whole world and
has no intention to establish friendly relations between
Japan and Manchukuo, there is a possibility of a collision.
The future will thus witness many complications in Japan's
Soviet relations. For the settlement of all pending
questions between Japan and Soviet Russia it will be
necessery for Japan to provide sufficient military force
to provide against Soviet armaments in the Far East,
and the replenishment of Japenese military forces is
therefore considered most urgent.
b. Relations between Japan and the United States.
The Washington treaty was set up in the name of
peace by the United States and Great Britain with the
objeot of checking Japan's development of the Asiatic
continent. The Japanese people realize this. In other
words

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
words, with the abrogation of the Anglo-Japanese al-
lianoe and the abandonment of the Lansing-Ishii agree-
ment Great Britain and the United States have tried
to place restrictions upon Japan's future activities
through the conclusion of the Four Power and Nine Power
Treaties. Both Great Britain and the United States have
caused Japan to accept the 5-5-3 naval ratio in order
to restrict the operations of the Japanese navy. In so
doing they attempted to maintain and exercise a voice
in Far Eastern questions. This fact is clearly mentioned
in the April number of Foreign Affairs in which Mr. Walter
Lippmann writes an article under the heading of "Coopera-
tion between Great Britain and the United States". In
this article Mr. Lippmann remarks among other things that
Great Britain placed restrictions upon Frence and then
upon Germany for the safety of Great Britain. In a
similar manner the United States wants to plece restrictions
upon Japan and Soviet Russi& and other countries in favor
of imperialism. Great Britain's policy was effected
through the conclusion of the Locerno treaty and the es-
tablishment of the League of Nations. In Asia the policy
of the United States was effected by the conclusion of
the Washington treaty. The Manchurien incident was ap-
parently a test as to whether such a restriction was
effeotive. The recent declaration of Germany to
strongthen her military forces has brought about a crisis
in the organization of the European league now in force.
In continuance Mr. Lippmann remarks that Great Britain
and other European countries are unable to bear respon-
sibility in the Far East. America is thus placed in a
position

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.98)
- 14-
position to face Japan single-handed. Consequently the
United States is obliged to reoonsider her policies in
the Far East.
Upon the outbreak of the Manchurian incident former
Secretary of State Stimson lodged a protest with Japan
action
contending that Japan's- in Manchukuo was a violation of
the provisions of the Nine Power Treaty. In the Shanghai
incident Stimson is reported to have decided to make an
armed intervention. It is not difficult to conjecture
what are the real motives of the United States. At the
time of the Shanghai incident the Japanese navy was
superior to the American navy. Stimson upon hearing the
advice of Admiral Pratt abandoned his intention of armed
intervention because he was aware that the American navy
was inferior to the Japanese navy at that time. Since
then the Japenese navy, as compared with the American navy,
has been reduced considerably in strength. The United
States is now increasing its navy in accordance with the
naval treaties. Bythe end of next year, 1936, Japan's
naval strength will fall to 80% as against the American
navy. By 1939 Japan's navy will be 63% of the American
navy. By the year 1939, when the American navy will be
strengthened up to treaty limits, it is a question whether
the United States will remain silent regarding the situation
in the Far East. In that eventuality China is very likely
to start anti-Japanese agitations again with the backing
of the United States and Europe. This is the reason why
the Japanese navy is replenishing itself in order to
strengthen Japan's national defenses in the future. Speak-
ing practically the United States is engaged in strengthening
her

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.99)
- 15 -
her naval and military forces because she has learned
a lesson from the Manchurian and Shanghai incidents.
In the future when the American navy will have been
strengthened up to the treat: limit it will be neces-
sary for Japan to provide a sufficient strength against
the American navy, otherwise no one can tell what will
occur. In pursuance of her traditional policy in the
Far East no one can tell what pressure the United States
may bring upon Japan's continental policy. Future
America-Japan relations will encounter a severe crisis
with America's policy of naval building.
c. Relations between Japan and China.
In China uneasiness still prevails due to disputes
among war lords. China comprises twenty-four provinces,
but orders issued by Chiang Kai Shek are carried out in
only eight of them. Other provinces are, practically
speaking, independent states. Sweeping operations by the
Nanking Government against communists are far from
successful. Communists in China, in cooperation with
Soviet Russia, are trying to undermine the present ad-
ministration. Soviet Russia is also trying to secure
rights and interests in China. Under the circumstances
there is a fear that China may be partitioned among the
powers or placed under international control. In such
an eventuality eastern Asia would be thrown into utter
confusion. Japan, which is endeavoring to maintain
Asiatio stability, oannot remain passive. Unfortunately
China is relying upon the United States and the European
powers. Conditions on the Asiatic continent are such as they
were in l9ll when outer Mongolia declared independence
and

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
and was placed under Russia's influence the following
year. In fact outer Mongolia is regarded as a territory
of Soviet Russia. Further south Russia is constructing
a railway and the day will not be far distant when this
region will become a second outer Mongolia. Tibet
declared her independence under the influence of Great
Britain in l903. Tibet is said to be following in the
same direction as Mongolia. Thus but one-third of
China is under the control of the Nanking Government.
In the Manchurian incident China learned a lesson
that reliance upon the United States was fruitless, and
China is now suffering considerably on uccount of America's
silver policy. China is thus obliged to adopt a pro-
Japanese policy. Cuite recently Japan and China elevated
their legations to the status of embassies with the view
to promote friendly relations between the two countries,
but in North China actions taken by China were quite
unfriendly. Thus the present North China situation has
been brought about. In the present pamphlet details of
the North China situation cannot be covered.
Regarding China's regular army it may be mentioned
that she has about 2,000,000 men, including those of
local leaders. Many of these, however, will desert when
they are offered good terms outside the army. The Chinese
army thus lacks rigid control. It is composed of the
following:

The subjugation of communists in China is a serious
internal guestion of the Nanking Government. The main
force of communists who have joined hands with Szechuan
Provinoe is so powerful that Chiang Kai Shek will be
unable to subjugate them. Though the communists are hard
pressed by the Government expeditionary forces, which are
superior in number, they are as active as before. This
1s because agricultural communities which have suffered
at the hands of the military in the past are now support-
ing the communists and because Soviet Russia is also
supporting them directly or indirectly in pursuance of
its policy of bolshevizing the Far East. If the com-
munists should establish their headquarters in Szechuan
Province, northwestern China will ultimately be placed
under their control and the result will be that China
will be totally bolshevized. In fact China will be
placed under the direct control of Soviet Russia. Lenin
once announced that the communizing of the world would be
decided in the East. Borodin emphasized the importance
of taking possession of Szechuan Province. All this
shows what the policy of Soviet Russia is toward China.
China, especially the Nanking Government, lacks sufficient
strength to withstand the territorial aggression of Soviet
Russia. The situation is such that the status of China
is endangered, and Japan stands to suffer thereby. Japan
cannot therefore remain passive regarding China. Japan
ha

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.96)

has urged the importance of cooperation between Japan
and China by oalling the attention of the Chinese people
to the coming orisis in the Far East. Unfortunately
the central government of China has not yet awakened to
the gravity of the situation. Nothing can be more
regrettable.

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.99)
-19 -
5. True peace and the way for Japan.
International relations in the past have been,
in brief, marked by the merciless law of the survival of
the fittest and the struggle for existence, and by end-
less conflicts among self-interested states. This circum-
stance has naturally given rise to such chaotic conditions
as are now being witnessed, the weak or the dissatisfied
trying to break the existing state of affairs and to open
brighter prospects for themselves.
From this it may be surmised that no peace can
be brought about upon the earth by the establishment of
a hundred leagues of nations or the conclusion of a thousand
non-aggression pacts, as long as this injustice or irra-
tionality among nations, the fundamental cause of inter-
national disputes, remains unredressed. Some nations,
in possession of abundant resources and territories, thrive
by squeezing weak countries; other nations, lacking natural
resources and territories, suffer. There are also states
which cannot maintain their independent status on account
of the provisions of irrational international treaties.
To rectify the situation and to afford equal opportunity
to various states and nations and thereby to bring about
mutual prosperity is the way to establish real peace.
Rational distribution of territories, natural re-
sources and population, restraint of passion for power
in strong states, this should be the keynote of inter-
national peace. The human race is now confronted with
an extensive deadlook which has resulted from the culti-
vation of free competition in the past. If some do not
awaken

=== Page 347 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
awaken to this fact and persist in dreaming of the
fulfilment of their ambitions, they may call down upon
themselves the denunciation of the whole world.
Now, what is the position of Japan at this moment,
and what is the situation in the Far East? The population
of Japan ranks foremost in density, 170 per square kilo-
meter. In Japan proper the population increases at the
rate of 900,000 a year, and if overseas territories are
added the annual increase is at the rate of 1300,000.
This is unique in the world. Within twenty years the
population of Japan will reach something like 100,000,000.
The scarcity of important products compels Japa-
nese to import the greater part of their daily necessi-
ties, iron, oil, cotton, wool, etc., though they are able
to obtain their food in their own dominion at present.
If this situation continues, the Japanese nation will be
exposed to starvation. Hence the only way open to the
Japanese is emigration, which might solve the population
question to some extent, and the encouragement of in-
dustry and commerce, which might supplement the paucity
of natural resources. Now Japanese emigration has been
more or less shut out in America, Australia, Canada, and
Brazil, while Japanese goods are being checked in their
advance by the imposition of high tariffs or import quotas.
On the other hand, the Asiatic continent, which nurtures
half the population of the world, has become the prey
of the powers in their contest for the acquimition of
territories, so that all Asia ercept Japan, China, and
Siam have been turned into colonies of the Euvopean and
American

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.99)
-21 -
Amerioan states, and the only Asiatic state which
plays a leading role is Japan.
China is being squeezed by the powers but she has
not yet awakened to the situation. She still follows
the traditional policy of relying upon distant coun-
tries and opposing near neighbors. Under the circum-
stances it is not impossible Chin& will ultimately be
converted into a territory of some of the powers or be
placed under international control.
Asia is the life-line for the Asiatic race but
possesses only secondary importance for the European
race. There is no reason why Japan should allow foreign
nations to squeeze China. Equal opportunity is what we
must insist upon. A certain power does not recognize
the principle of the open door and free opportunity on
its own continent, but intends to apply these principles
to the Far East. Such is not the way calculated to
bring about ideal world peace.
The birth of Manchukuo and Japan-Manchukuo co-
operation has a deep significance in correcting the world
policy of the Powers and as a foundation stone for the
realization of moral peace which aims at universal pros-
perity. It may also give the power of self-regeneration
to the Far East and constitute a step forward for the
realization of the lofty ideal just referred to.
Any misgiving as to ulterior motives on the part
of Japan after the recent North China trouble arises from
non-appreciation of the real motive of Japan's Far
Fastern policy. The cause of the present state of affairs

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.99)
- 22 -
in North China is China's policy of duplicity,
friendly on the one hand and on the other unfriendly.
Japan hopes that China will awake anu cooperate with
a view to establishing a real peace in the Far East,
thereby bringing about mutual prosperity. So long as
China retains her traditional policy of relying upon
the United States and Europe Japan will be obliged
to beat her into a realization of the situation.
Fortunately there are signs that some Chinese are
now beginning to undetand Japan's true motives and
are advocating cooperation with Japan. Certain sec-
tions of the various powers, especially of Great
Britain and the United States, are now beginning
to understand Japan's fair and reasonable insistence
for the establis.ment of un i.eal peace in the Far
Cast. This is a cause for hope. Japan hopes that
foreign nations will lay aside their mistaken idea of
Asia as a colony and adopt a policy of recognizing
Asia as for the asiatics. However certain powers are
trying to increase their navies far beyond the Japa-
nese naval strength in order to bring pressure to
bear upon Japan in Pacific and Far Eastern questions.
Some westerners resident in the Far East are
afraid that the influence of the white man might be
expelled from the Far East by Japan, and say that
England and America must cooperate to resist Japan's
advance. There are some who say that if the political
situation in Europe and America should be stabilized
and internal policies of the United States crowned
with success, then it would be possible for the United
States

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.98)
- 23 -
States to put down Japan. If any intervention
should be made from such ideas, the Far East
would be plunged into chaotic condition. Japan
should equip herself with the means of defending
the Far East in order to preclude any such con-
tingency.
:l1 the people of the earth are brothers.
They should love and assist one another. Ugly
ambitions of hegemony should be eschewed so that 
paradise of justice and virtue may be realized on
earth. To be guided by fairness and uprightness
and thereby to bring about ideal peace is the policy
of the Empire of Japan. Such is the great mission
to be performed by the Japanese race for the sake of
mankind. The only way to save the wretched condi-
tion of the world is that nations lay aside selfish
policies and meterialistic aims ani rectify abuses
and injustices among all nations on the basis of
brotherhood. Mutual prosperity and happiness would
then be brought about. Fortunately the policy of
the Empire of Japan is aimed at promoting the happi-
ness of mankind and thus bringing about ideal peace.
No country has so high a mission as Japan in saving
the world, in which at the present time nations
stand against each other. Japan desires earnestly
to bring prosperity and happiness to all mankind
by clearing away all abuses within the limits of our
country on the one hand, and on the other to help
woak

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.98)
-24 -
weak nations in the Far East which are now suffering
from the abusive policies of foreign countries.
The establishment of world peace is Japan's aim.
This must be the road for us.
Printed September 1, 1935.
Published September 5, 1935.
War Department Newspaper Section.

and is not identified with any army faction.

1.0
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
No.1450, dated August 23, 1935, on the subject of the
0C01 5- 1935
assassination of Major General Nagata, the Director
FILD
of the Military Affairs Bureau of the War Office, by
Lioutenant-Colonel Aizawa, and to report the following
developments of the affair.
As was indicated in the despatch referred to above,
F/FG.
the general opinion at the time of the assassination was
that General Hayashi, the Minister for War, should not
bo

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
be forced to resign beoruse of the incident, but shoudd
be permitted to carry out his plans, inaugurated before
the assassination, for "unification" of the Army and
the institution of more rigid control over the Army
personnel. After consult tion with the Premier and
other Governmental leaders, therefore, General Hayashi
decided not to resign immediately, but instead celled
a series of conferences of war councillors, commanders
of divisions and garrisons, directors of the military
schools, and chiefs of bureaux in the Ministry, in order
to expound to them his ideas as to enforcement of stricter
control. General Hayashi is an exponent of the so-called
"unification" of the Japanese Army, by which is meant the
unification of the Army under a central control, without
division into warring cliques and without interest or
concern in political affairs at home or abroad. The pro-
ponents of the "unification" plan believe (and with rea-
son) that if the Army dabbles in national politics or
social policies, it will soon become divided into such
opposing factions thst its effectiveness as a fighting
unit will be greatly impaired. It was, therefore, to
carry out his plans for better control of the Army that
General Hayashi continued in office as Minister for War,
after he should, according to ancient Japanese custom,
have resigned in order to indicate to the nation his
sense of responsibility for the assassination of Major
General Nagata. It is probable that Admiral Okada, the
Premier, also influenced General Hayashi to remain in
office, as in Japan a change of one of the defense
ministers

=== Page 355 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
minfsters may mean the downfall of the entire Cabinet.
At the first conference with the division and
garrison commanders, held on August 6, 1935, General
Hayashi issued his instructions to the Army, which
were officially announced by the War Office to be as
follows:
"Just at a time when the emergency
situation at home and abroad necessttated
the strengthening of the bonds uniting
the army, the assassination of Lieutenant-
General Nagata by Lieutenant-Colonel Saburo
Aizawa created an unprecedented situation
in the army. The disturbance thus caused
must have troubled the Throne, for which
there are no words adequate to express our
regret. Regardless of motives, such an oc-
currence is a blot on the glorious history
of the Imperial army.
"I, as War Minister, feel my responsi-
bility most keenly. Although I am convinced
that the incident could not have moved the
army as a whole one inch, the shock caused
at such a time cannot be left to do its
work on the mind of the army. Every caution
is necessary and every effort must be made
to strengthen discipline and unity throughout
its renks.
"Unity and discipline are the life of
the army. officers must take full cognizance
of their important position as the bulwarks
of the nation. Every soldier must realize
his duty to the Throne. Every gesture must
be ordered acoording to the rules of the army.
officers must be careful in their speech and
action so that the prestige of the army should
not be compromised and army secrets let out.
They must have clear eyes to sift out wild
rumors and suppress what are false.
"The heads of army units must be kind
and patient in their dealings with their sub-
ordinates but if after every effort their
subordinates commit offenses and are unrepentant
they should be firm in their punishment. Sub-
ordinates must be respeotful to their superiors
and even in the expression of their firm con-
victions must not forget the all-important
rules of discipline. The army, in brief, must
be united from head to foot by the consciousness
of its high mission to serve the nation and
Emperor."
There

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
There is nothing notable in these instructions,
exoept the mention of the faot that the Army's mission
is to "serve the nation and the Emperor", which may run
counter to the ideas of some sections of the Army, which
believe that the Army should lead the nation. The more
important instructions, according to the newspapers,
were issued by the Minister for War to the division and
garrison commanders in personal interviews. The OSAKA
MAINICHI AND TOKYO NICHI-NICHI of August 27, 1935, gives
the purport of these private instructions as follows:
"1. While Lieutenant-Colonel Aizawa's
real motive for killing Lieutenant-General
Nagata is still under investigation, it has
become clear that a personal motive was behind
the crime, and accordingly it will not serious-
ly affect the army. In view of the prevalence
of verious rumors, however, especially in the
country districts, utmost caution should be
taken against them, and such groundless rumors
should be striotly suppressed.
2. The strict system of control within the
army shall be maintained, and those who attempt
to disturb the system by forming horizontal or
vertical combinations shall be strictly punish-
ed.
3. In view of the recent tendencies, army
officers are coming in close touch with politi-
cians and other outside the military circle,
but such contacts should be avoided except in
unavoidable cases, for they are apt to disturb
the control within the army.
All commanders should be most disoreet
in their actions and utterances, and they should
have more concern about guiding their subordinates.
5. The army authorities shall take utmost care
in personal appointments and make closer the con-
nections between the central and local army au-
thorities."
The first instruotion above is undoubtedly intended
further to destroy rumors to the effect that the assassina-
tion of General Nagata was not caused by personal spite,
but

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
·but was due to a division of opinion in the Army. As
far as the mbassy has been able to ascertain, the im-
pelling reason for the assassination was probably per-
sonal, but there were also underlying reasons of more
general significance. Aizawa, the assassin, was known
as an introspective, morose man, thoroughly imbued with
the stern tenents of the samurai, and inclined to take
himeelf and the problems of his country too seriously.
He had at one time been associated with General Mazaki,
who was recently relieved of his post as Inspector-
General of Military Education, and probably resented
greatly this slight to his former chief. He had also
at one time in his career served in Formosa, and was un-
doubtedly greatly disgruntled at his recent transfer there
as military instructor in a school. These personal fact-
ors were probably the immediately impelling cause for
Aizawa's act, but it has also been found that he had been
influenced by certain pamphlets which had been circulated
among Army officers, accusing General Nagata of insin-
cerity and duplicity in connection with plans for in-
dustrial preparedness in Japan - a subject which was
General Nagata's particular hobby. These accusations
furnished the underlying motive for Aizawa's act - the
motive which, according to his perverted reasoning,
provided the patriotic ground for a deed impelled per-
haps primarily by personal spite.
The second instruotion is obviously aimed at the
formation of cliques within the Army. The authorities are
now endeavoring to dispel rumors that such cliques exist and
are disturbing control and discipline within the Army, but
thore

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
there is no doubt that such oliques do exist.
The third instruction is rather remarkable and has
attracted considerable attention in Japan. It advises
that Army officers keep to their own people within Army
circles and as far as possible avoid contaot with the
outside world. This, it would appear, has a double
purpose. On the one side it is intended to prevent the
medaling by Army officers in national and local politics,
and on the other it is intended to avoid the importation
into the Army of disturbing ideas obtained from civilians.
The eventual result might be, however, to render the Army
even more clannish, self-centered and fanatical than it
is at present.
The fourth instruction is obviously meant to curb
sensational utterances by Army officers - a step which
has been found desirable in other countries than Japan.
The second part of the instruction probably refers to the
need for better mental discipline among the younger officers.
The murder of General Nagata by Aizawa revealed a woeful
lack of discipline in the Army, but the lack is not of
collective and physical discipline, but rather of individual
and mental discipline - of the mental training which will
permit a subordinate officer willingly to defer to the
ideas of his superior officer, even though the subordinate
officer may not approve of those ideas. The Japanese Army
has in the past been subjected to severe mental training,
but it has been a training of precisely the opposite sort
to that indicated above. The training has been for the
purpose of teaching the young officers to aot on their own
initiative

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
initiative in times of emergenoy and to give them a
sense of self-reliance. Unfortunately, however, the
training has also given the young officers an exag-
gerated sense of the importance of their own half-
formed ideas. The state of mind which will impel a
subordinate to assassinate a superior and valuable
officer because the subordinate does not approve of
the superior's ideas and plans, or because he is dis-
satisfied with a post to which he has been appointed,
indicates a need for a different kind of mental
training - which fact the higher authorities ap-
parently now recognize.
The fifth instruction refers to the fact that in
the past there has been a tendency to concentrate at
headquarters in Tokyo all the best military talent,
as well as the officers who have the most influential
social or other affiliations, while men of lesser
ability or of less influence have been posted with the
provinoial divisions. It is now proposed to distribute
the outstanding officers inthe provincial divisions as
well as in the capital, in crder to raise the status of
the provincial divisions, to maintain closer contact
between the central and local Army units, and to develop
the consciousness of an Imperial Army, rather than the
sense of separate units of an Army. Steps toward this
ond were taken at the time of the August 1 transfers,
when an unusually large number of outstanding officers
was sent to the divisions in Manchuria. It is possible
a12

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
also that this policy may be useful in checking the
independent activities of the Kwantung Army, which
at times in the past has appeared to regard itself
almost as a separate entity, rather than as a part of
the Imperial Army.
The principal newspapers in Tokyo generally ap-
proved thoroughly of General Hayashi's instructions to
the Army and welcomed his decision to stay in office
until his plans for the "unification" of the Army had
been completed. The YOMIURI on August 27, 1935,
however, editorially criticized the system of military
education, which divoreed the Army from current social
trends and tended to breed fanatics such as Lieutenant-
Colonel Aizawa. The newspaper hoped that General
Hayashi would undertake also to correct this fault.
Having issued his instructions concerning control
of the Army and to that extent at least having carried
out his program of reform, General Senjuro Hayashi
today (September 4, 1935) tendered to the Premier his
formal resignation as Minister for War, giving as the
reason therefor his responsibility for the assassination
of General Nagata. His resignation was accepted and
General Yoshiyuki Kawashima, Supreme War Councillor,
111
was appointed as his successor. General Kawashima, who
is 57 years of age, was educated at the Military Academy
and the Military Staff College, after which he was sent
to Germany to study military affairs. He later served
at the Japanese Embassy in Berlin as Assistant Military
Attaohe

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.98)
-9-
Attache; has twice served in Chosen (recently in com-
mand of the Japanese troops in Chosen); and has several
times served in the Ministry of War in Tokyo. He is
known to be a steady, conservative ofricer, resembling
General Hayashi in his ideas and habits of thought. Ho
is not given to wild flights of fancy, but is a sober,
slow thinker, inclined to consult with those about him
before taking any important action. The general opinion
seems to be that he was appointed to the position to
oarry out the plans of reform inaugurated by General
Hayashi. As his appointment does not imply any change
in the administration of the Army or any alteration
in the military policies, it does not appear that the
change in War Ministers will have any political reper-
cussions.
The opinion of competent observers is that the
continuation of the policies of General Hayashi means
that the more conservative and solid element in the
Japanese Army will remain in power for some time.
Respectfully yours,
Edenin L aalle
Edwin L. Neville,
Chargé d'Arfaires ad interim.
